The full battery passed on the lab build of S4, all 17 suites, leaving no /tmp/karvi-<uid> or /var/tmp/karvi-<uid>.

55. An explicit env:NAME cell (2026-10-10) #

The ROADMAP's Next item 1: a credential CSV cell that names an environment variable explicitly, needs no flag, and fails with its own code when the variable is unset, where a cell naming an unset variable under the indirection flags stays a literal (docs/CREDENTIAL-CSV.md, section 6).

The evidence. A lab build of 35a0e55, every place under a short lab directory; the target the fake device on 127.0.0.1:2223, accepting netops and S3cret. A credential CSV whose catch-all row's password cell is NETPASS_NYC, under env-indirection.password = true:

NETPASS_NYC=S3cret exported   exit 0
NETPASS_NYC unset             authentication_failed: SSH authentication failed for netops at 127.0.0.1:2223: …   exit 108
the dry run, unset            "status": "bound", matched_on the row's line 2, exit 0

The name is sent as the password, and nothing names the variable. Two inventory devices, one whose row's password is blank:

credential_password_missing: 1 of 2 targets failed credential resolution: name:bad (password is required for the selected authentication policy policy=default backend=site-creds)
exit 6

One device's credential failure refuses the job at planning, before any connection, and the dry run says the same. Resolution runs in the client planner alone: the daemon receives sealed grants and never reads a credential CSV.

What it gains. A file that names variables fails before any device is reached when one is unset, where today the name is sent as the password: failed logins, on some devices lockouts, and nothing naming the cause. It needs no flag. It defines the code for an unset variable and the lookup that the credential helper's values, the ROADMAP's next item, will reach. It waits on nothing.

Its issues, one at a time: the cell's form; the CSV's three env-indirection flags beside it; the code, when it is said, and what it names; the lookup, the door the helper's values will come through; and, found on the way, a field source nothing reads, keys a backend's type never reads, and a backend type with no driver.

Issue 1, the cell's form, agreed. On the lab build, the bare word Xray in each result column, the fake device expecting it in that field, the field's flag off and on, and a variable Xray unset and set to other:

Cell Flag off, unset Flag off, set Flag on, unset Flag on, set
username Xray sent, exit 0 Xray sent, exit 0 Xray sent, exit 0 other sent, exit 108
password Xray sent, exit 0 Xray sent, exit 0 Xray sent, exit 0 other sent, exit 108
enable_password Xray sent, exit 0 Xray sent, exit 0 Xray sent, exit 0 other sent, exit 107

A password env:Secret1, the cell holding it as written, logs in with the flag off and on. A cell of the three result columns, username, password, and enable_password, whose text with its surrounding white space trimmed begins with env:, the prefix's case ignored, is a reference to the variable its rest names, with the field's flag or without it; a plain cell stays as the table shows. The name is a portable variable name, [A-Za-z_][A-Za-z0-9_]*, its case kept, checked for every row when the file loads: anything else after the prefix (nothing, a hyphen, a space) is credential_csv_row_invalid, naming the line and the column and never the cell, since a file's form is checked whole (docs/CREDENTIAL-CSV.md, section 4). The variable is read for the selected row alone, at planning: a set variable gives its value, an unset one an error that refuses the run (issue 3). A value that begins with env: has no escape: it goes into a variable, and the cell names the variable. Not taken: an escape (env::text, a second rule for what a variable already carries); ${NAME} (read as an expansion inside a value, which karvi does not do); a reference only when the rest is a valid name, else a literal (env:NETPASS-NYC sent as a password, the hazard this item removes); the prefix in lower case alone (ENV:NETPASS sent as a literal, the hazard again); a selector or credkey cell (they choose the row).

Issue 2, the CSV's three env-indirection flags, agreed. Under a field's flag a plain cell that names a set variable is replaced by its value, and one that names no set variable is sent as written (issue 1's table, its last two columns); the flags' reason was a file mixing names and real passwords, which env:NAME gives cell by cell, an unset variable failing. A csv backend takes no env-indirection key: env-indirection.username, .password, or .enable-password on one is refused at load, config_credential_backend_key_unsupported (exit 2), its message saying that a cell names a variable as env:NAME. A cell of the three result columns is a literal, sent as written whatever variables are set, or env:NAME, the variable's value or an error; a formula reading a csv backend takes its row by the same rule. A file used under the flags writes its names as env:NAME. The env, Redis, and Vault backends keep their flags, the credential helper's to fold. Not taken: the flags kept beside the cell (a file under two rules, the hazard kept for every plain cell under a flag); the flags kept with an unset name failing (every plain cell of the column a name, no literal beside one, which env: says per cell); the flags ignored on a csv backend (a file under them sending each name as a password, nothing said).

Issue 3, the code, when it is said, and what it names, agreed. On the lab build, a variable set and empty under each field's flag today: the username cell gives credential_incomplete and the password cell credential_password_missing, at planning, exit 6; the enable cell reaches the device with no enable secret, privilege_failed, exit 107. A failure at planning leaves no job directory and no audit event, and is said on standard error. A new code, credential_env_missing, category credential, exit 6, named as the other missing fields are and not after the CSV, so that the credential helper's other readers can say it. It is said when a device's selected row is resolved, at planning in the client, before any connection: the job is refused, every device it stops listed, and the dry run says the same. It stops the device, which never moves on to a later row, a later backend, NETUSER and NETPASS, or the prompt (section 4). A variable of a row no device took is not read, and config validate, which never opens the file, says nothing. A variable set and empty is missing too: an env: cell asks for a value, and an empty one comes from export NETPASS= or a failed substitution. The message names the backend, the file, the line, the column, and the variable, the env: prefix marking the cell as a reference: VAR1 is not set, or VAR1 is empty:

credential_env_missing: 1 of 2 targets failed credential resolution: name:bad (credential backend site-creds: /dev/shm/k1/creds.csv:3 password: VAR1 is not set policy=default backend=site-creds)

A password written before this rule that begins with env: and a valid name has that rest printed once, on standard error, a cost the operator accepted. A cell whose rest is not a valid name is no reference, and issue 1's refusal names its line and column alone. Not taken: one code per backend (credential_csv_env_unset, another code for each later reader); an empty variable as set (a blank enable secret reaching the device, the failure naming the field and not the cell); the line and column without the name (the operator's remedy one look away, the name being a reference's); every row's variables checked at load (a run on one site's devices needing every site's variables exported).

At the operator's questions, blank cells, NET*, and keys. On the lab build, every combination of blank and filled result cells in one row, the fake device asking for the enable secret, and this host's OpenSSH as a Linux target holding the operator's key:

Row (username, password, enable_password) Result
all filled exit 0
the enable cell blank privilege_failed at the device, exit 107; credential_enable_missing at planning under requires-enable = true; exit 0 on a device that starts privileged
the password cell blank, whatever else credential_password_missing at planning, exit 6
the username cell blank the operator's own name, from the password database through the backend's transform (USER beside it unread)
all blank credential_csv_row_invalid at load, exit 6
a blank cell, its NET* variable exported as without it: NETUSER, NETPASS, and NETENABLE unread
no row matching, NET* exported the platform's fallback, builtin-env-fallback
a Linux target, the password cell blank credential_password_missing, exit 6, the operator's key never reached
a Linux target, a wrong password authentication_failed, exit 108, no key offered
a Linux target, no row matching the fallback's keys, builtin-operator-keys, auth publickey

NET* is read by the platform's fallback alone, after every backend of the policy has not answered, for a CSV when no row matches; a cell reads it as env:NETPASS. A row holds no key, so it needs a password; a key is the fallback's keys word (the linux built-ins'), and a device takes it only when no backend answers for it. The env: cell changes none of this: it decides where a filled cell's value comes from. Found: [credential-transform.NAME] is documented nowhere but its one error code.

Issue 4, the lookup, agreed. In the tree, the NET* fallback reads the environment through the resolver's input provider, TTYInput's os.LookupEnv, set by the planner; the CSV reads os.LookupEnv itself, envindirect defaulting to it when the backend's own lookup is unset, which nothing in production sets; the env, Redis, and Vault backends call os.LookupEnv themselves. Every implementation of the provider's lookup, two in production and four in tests, ignores its context and returns no error. The credential helper's values are held as secrets and never put into the process environment a child such as ssh inherits, so a reader of the process environment cannot see them: the door is karvi's own lookup. The resolver's one lookup is its input provider's, the one the NET* fallback reads, and it reaches every backend on credentials.ResolveRequest, a function from a name to its value and whether it is set, set where the resolver builds a request, a formula's source taking the same request. The CSV's env: cell reads it; the backend's own lookup and envindirect's default to os.LookupEnv go, and a request without a lookup reads no variable. Today the provider reads the process environment, so nothing changes for the operator: env:VAR1 finds what the shell exported. The helper, later, adds its values behind the provider, and every reader finds them there; the env, Redis, and Vault backends move onto the same lookup then. The provider's lookup takes a name and gives the value and whether it is set, its context and error gone with the fallback's unreachable error branch: the helper runs as a step before resolution, and the lookup reads what it left. envindirect holds the env: rule, the cell recognised, the name's grammar checked at load, and the lookup and credential_env_missing at resolution; its flag function, with no caller once the CSV takes no flags, goes. Not taken: the CSV reading os.LookupEnv (the helper's values out of its reach, the cell and the fallback reading through two doors); the helper's values put into the process environment (every child, ssh among them, inheriting them); a lookup given to each backend when it is built (the backends are built before the planner sets the provider, and one built without it reads nothing, or the process environment unseen); the provider itself on the request (a backend able to prompt); the context and error kept for the helper (it runs before resolution, not inside a lookup).

Issue 5, found: a field source nothing reads, agreed. Each backend attaches to its credential a note per field saying where the value came from (the CSV creds.csv:3 password, or env:NETPASS_NYC under the flag; the env backend the variable; .cloginrc its file, line, and pattern; the fallback builtin-env-fallback), and the ROADMAP asked what it says for an env: cell. Nothing reads it: no manifest, grant, command record, audit event, or dry run carries it, and in the code it is written in 23 places in 8 files and read by one test of the CSV backend; its Transform part is never set. credentials.FieldSource, the credential's FieldSources, every backend's construction of it, envindirect.Source, and the documents' sentences about it go. Where an env: cell's value came from is said already: matched_on names the file and the line, the cell at that line says env:NAME, and an unset or empty variable names itself (issue 3). Found beside it and removed with it: the resolver's finalize takes a promptable parameter it never reads (_ = promptable), since the public tree began, its five callers passing true or false for nothing. Not taken: a reader given to it (the dry run or each record saying where each field came from, which the row says and matched_on names); the field kept for the credential helper (an unread value waits for no reader; if the helper's design needs one, it brings the reader and the field together).

Issue 6, found: keys a backend's type never reads, agreed. On the lab build config validate accepts any credential-backend key on any type: a Redis backend with Vault's mount, a formula with a path, an env backend with address, a Vault backend with Redis's tls, a cloginrc backend with env-indirection.password, a csv backend with token-env, each valid: true, exit 0. The registry admits every type's keys on every type, and the load refuses by type only scope and required on a type that is not a file's (config_credential_backend_scope_unsupported) and the CSV's reader keys on another type (config_credential_backend_key_unsupported). A key its type ignores is a setting the operator takes to be in force that is not. What each type reads, from the code that builds it, beside type and transform, which every type reads:

Type Its keys
env username-var-template, password-var-template, enable-var-template, env-indirection.*
cloginrc scope, path, required
csv scope, path, required, mode, delimiter, mappings.<field>, mandatory-fields
redis address, database, key-template, tls, ca-file, client-cert-file, client-key-file, auth-username-env, auth-password-env, connect-timeout, env-indirection.*
vault address, namespace, mount, path-template, token-env, ca-file, client-cert-file, client-key-file, connect-timeout, username-field, password-field, enable-field, env-indirection.*
formula username-template, password-source
sqlite none (issue 7)

The list is held per type beside the registry, and any other key on a table of that type is refused at load, config_credential_backend_key_unsupported (exit 2), naming the types that take it: address applies to the redis and vault backend types only for credential-backend.e.address at …. The two partial checks are cases of the one rule, issue 2's refusal among them with its hint, and config_credential_backend_scope_unsupported folds into the one code: scope on a Redis backend is a key its type does not take. The ROADMAP's Credentials item held it as policing every backend type's keys by type. Not taken: a warning (the setting not in force left in the file); the CSV's flags alone refused (every other type keeping the gap); the scope code kept (two codes for one rule).

Issue 7, found: the sqlite type, which has no driver, agreed. On the lab build a sqlite backend with its six keys (table, operator-column, username-column, password-column, enable-column, busy-timeout) is valid: true, and a run reaching it fails at planning, sqlite_backend_unavailable ("the current portable build has no SQLite driver"), exit 6: the resolver builds a placeholder that answers that failure, and no code reads the keys. An unregistered key on a backend table is refused, config_unknown_key, before the table's type is checked. The type is removed from the code: type = "sqlite" is refused at load as any unknown type is, config_credential_backend_type_unsupported (exit 2); the six keys leave the registry and are refused as removed on any backend table, as daemon.socket is (chapter 49), config_key_removed, removed in v0.30.0, one hint saying that the sqlite type had no driver and is removed and that nothing read the key; the placeholder, sqlite_backend_unavailable, and its rows in the error codes go. A SQLite store may come back as a ROADMAP item, holding device or operator data or both, with its own type, keys, and driver. Not taken: the type kept with no keys (a backend that validates and can never answer); the keys left unregistered without a removed row (a refusal naming the key and nothing of why); a driver now (the later store, designed with its own keys).

Built. In three sections. 56. The credential helper (2026-10-11) #

The ROADMAP's Next item 1: a configured executable whose output sets values for karvi's own use, which every credential reader finds through the resolver's one lookup (chapter 55, issue 4). Its first part moves the env, Redis, and Vault backends' reads of the environment onto that lookup.

The evidence. A lab build of 9b722da, every place under a short lab directory; the target the fake device on 127.0.0.1:2223, accepting netops, S3cret, and the enable secret en; a Redis server and a Vault server, each a short script speaking the protocol and logging what it was sent, holding the operator netops's record, whose password field is the variable name NETPASS_NYC, each backend under env-indirection.password = true:

Case Result
env, Redis, Vault: NETPASS_NYC exported exit 0
env, Redis, Vault: NETPASS_NYC unset the text NETPASS_NYC sent as the password, authentication_failed, exit 108
Redis: auth-password-env's variable unset redis_auth_missing: … Redis authentication environment variable is absent, exit 6, read after the connection
Redis: the same, set and empty AUTH sent with an empty password, redis_auth_failed, exit 6
Vault: token-env's variable unset, or set and empty vault_token_missing: … Vault token environment variable is absent, exit 6
Vault, a run through the daemon exit 0; the daemon's environment holds COLORTERM HOME LANG LOGNAME PATH TERM USER alone

The six reads call os.LookupEnv themselves, and the three packages have no tests. The Vault read through the daemon was the client's: a job's credentials are resolved in the client that plans it, whatever runs the job.

What it gains. The first part: the hazard chapter 55 removed from the CSV, a name sent as a password when its variable is unset, leaves the three backends that keep it; their messages name the variable; an empty variable is one rule where Redis sent an empty AUTH and Vault called it missing; and the three packages gain tests. It changes no value while the provider reads the process environment. The helper: a site's script fetches secrets at invocation, from a password manager, a vault's own client, or a keyring, into karvi's memory alone. Today a secret reaches karvi from a file at rest (a credential CSV, a .cloginrc), from the shell's environment (export, or eval "$(script)", which every program the shell starts inherits; karvi's own children receive HOME, USER, LOGNAME, PATH, and the allow-list), from the prompt (a terminal, once per field per invocation), or from Vault or Redis, whose token is itself a variable. The unattended collection, crun --all --no-daemon under a timer or cron, needs a backend that answers without a prompt (docs/COLLECTION.md, section 7), today a file at rest or a variable in the unit.

What it waits on. Nothing: the one lookup is the helper's door. The ROADMAP's question whether the daemon may run the helper for an unattended job is answered by the code: the daemon resolves no credential, and an unattended collection is a client, so the helper runs where resolution runs.

At the operator's questions. Nine, on the backends and the fallback, answered on the lab:

  • A Redis or Vault backend is asked only when a policy's backend-sequence names it; both defined and named by none, a device took NETUSER and NETPASS. Their variables hold the login to the store, not the device's credential: Vault always needs its token (token-env, default VAULT_TOKEN); Redis needs a variable only for a server that asks for a login, an open server, one with a password (auth-password-env), or one with users (auth-username-env beside it), each over TLS by default. The record, chosen by the operator's name after the backend's transform, gives the username, the password, and the enable password (Redis's hash fields username, password, enable_password; Vault's username-field, password-field, enable-field). Three devices through one backend made three Redis logins and reads, or three Vault reads, the answers merged into one grant.
  • The token is read when a device's sequence reaches Vault: a device answered by an earlier CSV row ran with VAULT_TOKEN unset, and config validate reads none. Unset, the device stops, and the job with it, at planning, with NETUSER and NETPASS exported and untried; a wrong token is vault_http_error (HTTP 403), exit 6; no record at the path is not found, the device moving on to the fallback.
  • A device's policy is the first matching [[credential-policy-map]] rule (an address-cidr rule by its longest prefix); its backend-sequence is the precedence, where a backend's answer ends the walk, its not finding moves on, and its failure stops the device. Then the platform's fallback: ["netvars", "prompt"] for the network built-ins, ["keys"] for linux. At a terminal, three devices and NETUSER exported asked Password for 3 targets: once, at planning, backend=interactive-tty on each.
  • NETUSER, NETPASS, and NETENABLE are read only when no backend of the device's policy answered, and never fill a field a backend's answer left blank: a CSV row with a blank enable cell, NETENABLE exported, reached the device and failed, privilege_failed: the device asked for an enable secret after "enable" and the credential has none, or under requires-enable was credential_enable_missing at planning. A store takes a NET* value by naming it: a CSV cell env:NETENABLE (a bare NETENABLE cell is sent as the text, and the device stayed at Router>), an env backend's enable-var-template = "NETENABLE". The prompt asks for an enable secret only under the platform's requires-enable, which no built-in sets: Enable password for d1:.
  • NETSUDO is read by nothing: sudo with a password is designed (docs/DESIGN.md, section 5) and not built, and goes to the ROADMAP's Later list at the operator's word.
  • matched_on is a credential's provenance, in each command record, the manifest, the grant, and --debug, and part of what merges two devices' grants. Its source is a file's path, a store's record, or a built-in source's name; Redis's is the text redis-key-digest-not-implemented, in every record, and value_digest, declared beside it and in the credential package's schema, is set by nothing and read by nothing.
  • The templates' %s came from the specification, which gave key-template="netdo:credential:%s" and username-template = "svc.%s" beside configuration macros, %name% filled at load, and never said how the two meet. Each template is Go's printf given the operator's name: in a formula's username-template, the dry run named:
username-template Beside it The username
%s-adm netops-adm
svc svc%!(EXTRA string=netops)
svc-%d svc-%!d(string=netops)
%s-%s, %s-50% refused: config_macro_undefined: undefined macro %s-%, … %s-50%
%user% refused: undefined macro %user%
%user% [macros] user = "fixed" fixed%!(EXTRA string=netops)

A Redis key-template = "karvi:shared", one record for every operator, asked for karvi:shared%!(EXTRA string=netops) and a Vault path-template = "karvi/shared" for the same, neither found, the device on to the prompt.

Its issues, one at a time: the three backends' reads on the one lookup, and the operator's name in their templates; their env-indirection flags; then five found on the way, which the operator agreed to take in this chapter: auth-username-env read by nothing without auth-password-env (no AUTH was sent), the configuration's variable names unchecked at load (token-env = "VAULT TOKEN" and auth-password-env = "redis-pass" valid), Redis's matched_on.source, Vault's defaults filled in by Resolve while devices resolve in parallel (from the code), and the unread value_digest; then the helper's own: when it runs, the names it may set, its output's grammar, the executable's checks, environment, timeout, standard error, and exit, and its configuration beside the helper protocols operators know; and, found while designing them or at the operator's word, refusals said without colour at a terminal, the operator-keyed stores' answers read once and held, and TLS to the stores; and, found while building, an empty value, what a helper leaves running, and colour turned off for one invocation and never written into a file; and, found while writing its documents, the removed flags' hint.

Issue 1, the reads on the one lookup and %operator%, agreed. On the lab build, with the flags off: the env backend answers when any of its variables is set, a blank password credential_password_missing and a blank username credential_incomplete, all three set and empty credential_incomplete, none set the fallback; Redis given auth-username-env naming an unset variable sent AUTH with an empty username; Vault with no token-env read VAULT_TOKEN. The rule, in two parts. The operator's name. %operator% is a built-in macro, the operator's login name from the password database, filled when the configuration loads in every value of every layer, as a site's macro is, the digest and config show the filled values. Seven keys keep it as written for the backend: the env backend's username-var-template, password-var-template, and enable-var-template, Redis's key-template, Vault's path-template, a formula's username-template, and a policy's username-template; there it is filled when the backend is asked, with the name after the backend's operator transform, the key being filled deciding, so a site's macro holding %operator% keeps it in one of those keys. Every %operator% is replaced, as text, and a template without one is used as written: karvi:shared, SRV_USER. %s is text; the defaults are karvi:credential:%operator% and karvi/%operator%. [macros] operator is refused at load, config_macro_name_reserved (exit 2), and %Operator% is an ordinary macro. A store's answer is data and is never expanded: a username %operator% from a CSV cell or a Redis field is sent as written, as %s and %HOME% are today. The one lookup. The env backend's three variables, Redis's two, and Vault's token are read through ResolveRequest.LookupEnv, the resolver's one lookup, and os.LookupEnv leaves the three packages; a request without a lookup reads no variable. The env backend's rule stays. A variable the configuration names for Redis or Vault (the token, the password, the username when its key is set), unset or empty, is credential_env_missing (exit 6), read before any connection, naming the backend, the key, and the variable: credential backend v: token-env: VAULT_TOKEN is not set. redis_auth_missing and vault_token_missing retire into it in v0.30.0. The three packages gain tests, and OPERATIONS gains the Redis and Vault backends and the configuration's macros, which no document an operator reads describes. A configuration that still writes %s reads it as text, under the operator's stance on breaking changes: an env backend's variable KARVI_%s_PASSWORD is never set, a Redis key or Vault path finds no record, and a formula sends %s-adm. Not taken: %s kept (printf exposed, a fixed name broken); one value everywhere, the name at load (a backend's operator transform no longer reaching its templates); a placeholder outside the macro syntax, {operator} (two placeholder styles in one file); %user% (the device's username everywhere else); %s refused in the seven keys (a guard for an upgrade); a store's answer expanded (a password holding %operator% changed, a second reference form in data). Noted and left: a blank username from the env backend, Redis, or Vault is credential_incomplete, where a CSV row's or a .cloginrc's is the operator's own name.

Issue 2, the three backends' env-indirection flags, agreed. On the lab build, under a field's flag each of the three backends sent the name NETPASS_NYC as the password when the variable was unset (exit 108), and a Redis record's real password USER, the device accepting that text, was replaced by the value of the variable USER; without a flag a Redis password field env:NETPASS_NYC and a Vault enable field env:NETENABLE were sent as the text, authentication_failed and privilege_failed. Under a flag a field is a name or a value as the environment happens to say, and without one a store cannot name a variable, where the operator's rule is that a store takes a NET* value by naming it. The keys env-indirection.username, .password, and .enable-password leave the registry and are refused as removed in v0.30.0 on any [credential-backend.NAME], config_key_removed, as the sqlite keys are, the hint that a Redis or Vault field names a variable as env:NAME and an env backend's template names its variable; chapter 55's refusal of them on a csv backend is this one rule. A Redis or Vault record's username, password, or enable password field (Redis's fixed field names, Vault's username-field, password-field, and enable-field) whose value, trimmed, begins with env:, the prefix's case ignored, is a reference to the variable its rest names, read through the one lookup by the CSV's own functions, envindirect.Reference and Read; an unset or empty one is credential_env_missing, naming the backend, the record, the field, and the variable (credential backend r: karvi:credential:netops password: NETPASS_NYC is not set); any other value is a literal, and a password that begins with env: lives in a variable and is named. A reference whose rest is not a portable variable name is the record's form wrong, read at resolution and not at load: the store's own code for a malformed record, redis_malformed or vault_response_malformed (exit 6), their descriptions widened, naming the backend, the record, and the field and never the value. The env backend's flags go with nothing in their place: a variable's value is the value, env:X the text env:X, and its template names the variable (enable-var-template = "NETENABLE"). The three backends' indirect functions and fields and the resolver's flag reads go. Not taken: the flags kept (two rules for one need, the hazard kept); the flags kept with an unset name failing (every field of the column a name); no reference in a store (a store unable to name NETENABLE); env: in the env backend's values (a variable naming a variable, which its template does); a new code for a bad reference in a store (a third code where each store has one for a malformed record).

Issue 3, found: keys a backend's other keys leave unread, agreed. On the lab build each of these is valid: true: Redis's auth-username-env alone, read by nothing, no AUTH sent; a client certificate without its key, Redis's under TLS and Vault's, refused only when a device reaches the backend, redis_client_cert_key_unpaired and vault_client_cert_key_unpaired, exit 6; Redis's ca-file, client-cert-file, and client-key-file under tls = false, none of the files existing, unread and the run exit 0; a Vault ca-file beside an http:// address, read for a connection that uses no TLS, vault_tls, exit 6. Each is chapter 55's issue 6 again, a setting the operator takes to be in force that is not. A key that the backend's other keys leave unread is refused when the configuration loads, config validate included, under config_credential_backend_key_unsupported (exit 2), its description "a key its type does not read, or its other keys leave unread", the message naming what the key needs: Redis's auth-username-env without auth-password-env (is read only beside auth-password-env); Redis's or Vault's client-cert-file without client-key-file, or the reverse (is read only beside client-key-file); Redis's three TLS files under tls = false (is read only under tls = true); Vault's three TLS files with an address that is not https:// (is read only for an https address). redis_client_cert_key_unpaired and vault_client_cert_key_unpaired retire into it in v0.30.0, and their branches, which no configuration can reach, go. Whether a named file exists stays a question of the run: config validate opens no credential file. Not taken: the case found alone refused (the other four kept); a code of its own (chapter 55's one code for a key not in force); a warning; the files checked at load.

Issue 4, found: the configuration's variable names, agreed. On the lab build each of these is valid: true: Vault's token-env = "VAULT TOKEN", vault_token_missing with VAULT_TOKEN exported; token-env = "KARVI-TOKEN", the variable set by env(1) and dropped by the shell between it and karvi; token-env = "", silently VAULT_TOKEN; an env backend's password-var-template = "KARVI-%s-PASSWORD", its variable never arriving, and password-var-template = "", reading nothing; Redis's auth-password-env = "redis-pass". A name that is no portable variable name cannot reach karvi through a shell, where an env: cell's name is checked when the file loads. token-env, auth-username-env, and auth-password-env must each be a portable variable name, letters, digits, and underscores, not beginning with a digit; anything else, "" included, is refused when the configuration loads, config validate included, config_credential_backend_variable_invalid (exit 2), quoting the value, a name and no secret ("VAULT TOKEN" is not a variable name … for credential-backend.v.token-env at …); a key left out says none, or for Vault the default. An env backend's three templates are checked at load with each %operator% stood in by a letter (KARVI_%operator%_PASSWORD passes, KARVI-%operator%-PASSWORD and "" do not), and when the backend is asked with the operator's name after its transform: a name that is then no variable name (KARVI_j.smith_PASSWORD) stops the device at planning under the same code, naming the variable and the remedy, a transform's replace-chars, where today it is silently unset and the device moves on. Redis's key-template, Vault's path-template, and the two username-template keys name no variable and are not checked. Not taken: the names unchecked (a key that can never work, valid); the operator's name checked at load (a shared configuration refused for j.smith though no run of theirs reaches the env backend); "" kept as none or the default (two spellings); the computed name silently unset (the device on to NET* or the prompt, nothing said); config_credential_backend_key_unsupported (a key not in force, where this one is in force and names nothing).

Issue 5, found: Redis's matched_on.source, agreed. Every Redis credential on the lab build carries "source": "redis-key-digest-not-implemented", in each command record and in the manifest's grant, where three devices' credentials merged into one; Vault's names the path it read, v1/secret/data/karvi/netops. The specification described matched_on as the category, a lookup key's digest or safe value, the matched pattern, and the source record, and the Redis backend wrote a note that the digest was not done. A Redis credential's source is the key the backend read, its key-template with %operator% filled after the transform (karvi:credential:netops, or karvi:shared for a fixed key), as Vault's is its path; it goes wherever matched_on goes, the records, the grant, and --debug's matched_source=, and the devices a policy sends to one Redis backend read one key, so their grants merge as before. OPERATIONS's Redis and Vault sections say what source holds. Not taken: a digest of the key (no secret, and nothing would consume it); the field dropped for Redis (the one store whose records do not say where a credential came from); the address or database added (the record's backend names the backend).

Issue 6, found: Vault's defaults written during resolution, and the values beside them, agreed. On a lab copy of the tree, go test -race calling the Vault backend from eight goroutines, as the planning pool calls a backend for several devices, built as the resolver builds it for a table with no path-template, reported a data race: Resolve writes the default PathTemplate into the backend every device shares (backend.go:43 and :44), on every Vault run of two devices or more under the default; the value is the same each time, and the race is still undefined. Beside it, on the lab build, each of these is valid: true and silently the default: Redis's tls = "false", a string, TLS on and a TLS hello sent to a plain server, redis_unavailable; database = "2", no SELECT; database = -1, SELECT -1 sent; connect-timeout = "abc", and Vault's "0s" ("-1s" validating too), the default timeout, which Resolve then writes, the same race; Redis's address = 6380, an integer, redis_address_missing at resolution; Vault's token-env = 5, VAULT_TOKEN; an env template 1, reading nothing. The backend table's keys have no kinds at load, and the resolver's readers fall back to a default without a word. Each key of the env, Redis, and Vault types has a kind, checked when the configuration loads, config validate included: a string, but tls a boolean, database an integer of 0 or more, and connect-timeout a duration above zero; a wrong kind is config_type_error, an unparsable duration config_duration_error, a negative one config_duration_negative, and a negative database or a zero timeout config_value_out_of_range, each exit 2. Redis's and Vault's address is required and well formed, Redis's host:port, Vault's an http:// or https:// URL with a host, else config_credential_backend_address_invalid (exit 2), missing or malformed; redis_address_missing and vault_address retire into it in v0.30.0. The defaults are set once, where the resolver builds the backend (Vault's mount, path template, field names, token variable, and timeout; Redis's key template and timeout), and Resolve reads its backend and never writes it, its default branches gone and the race with them. The tests: Resolve leaves its backend as built (failing today without -race), a concurrent Resolve under -race, and each value above refused at load. Not taken: the race fixed alone, by a lock or the one default moved (the silent defaults kept); a warning (the setting not in force left in the file); connect-timeout = "0s" as no timeout (a read at planning that never ends); a code per key. At the operator's word, the optional ca-file and client certificate under TLS are to be revisited in this chapter.

Issue 7, found: the unread value_digest, agreed. credentials.Match declares ValueDigest (value_digest, omitted when blank) and the credential package's envelope schema lists it among a match's optional properties; no code sets it and nothing reads it, the command record's matched_on written by hand without it, so no grant, plan report, or package has carried it. It is the specification's lookup key digest, as Redis's placeholder was (issue 5). ValueDigest leaves credentials.Match and value_digest the schema's match definition. The credential package's counter stays 2: credkey joined the same definition as an optional property with no move, and an optional property no package ever carried leaves no byte of the wire changed, every package valid before valid after and the reverse. The package test that validates a package against the schema gains a match carrying value_digest, refused (additionalProperties: false), which fails today. Not taken: the counter moved (a change no package can show); the field kept for a later digest (an unread value waits for no reader).

Issue 8, when the helper runs, agreed. In the tree, credentials are resolved in the client alone, in three places: the planner, for command, run (its dry run and a run through the daemon included), and crun, its twin for a run sent to the daemon, and login's resolver; config validate, daemon, job, prune, and version resolve none, and the daemon resolves nothing. Chapter 55 left the lookup without an error, the helper a step before resolution whose values the lookup reads. A stream resolves per job: at a pseudo-terminal, a stream of two jobs, NETUSER exported and NETPASS not, asked Password for d1: at each --go. When a helper is configured, an invocation that resolves credentials runs it once, at its first credential step, before any backend or fallback is asked, and holds its values for the invocation, every lookup reading them; it runs whether or not a device ends up reading a variable, since configuring it is the operator's choice to run it, and an invocation that resolves no credential, and the daemon, never run it. A stream runs it at its first job's credential step and holds its values for every later job, and the first job after a --restart runs it again, so rotated secrets are fetched without leaving the stream; a stream that sends no job never runs it. A failure refuses that invocation, or that stream's job, at planning, before any device is reached (its code is issue 11's); nothing is held, the failed job is not sent again, and the next job the operator sends runs the helper again, as a command run again would; within one job it runs once, never per device, on a second planning pass, or as a retry. Not taken: per device, given the device's name (a script that answers per device is a credential backend, a type of its own); lazily at the first lookup (the lookup takes no error, and a failure would land on whichever device asked first); per job in a stream, as the prompt asks (a helper that unlocks a password manager asking at every --go); at the stream's start, before any line (an unlock asked for a stream that may send nothing); a failure held until --restart (a stream outlives the cause, and --restart empties the draft); the daemon running it (it resolves nothing). At the operator's word, the helper's values held for the invocation are to shape how the operator-keyed stores are asked: a Redis or Vault answer read once and held, not read again for each device of a thousand-device job, to be taken up in this chapter.

Issue 9, the helper's names, its configuration, and its failures, agreed. In the tree the one lookup is read for names of every kind: NETUSER, NETPASS, and NETENABLE by the fallback, any name by an env: cell or field, the env backend's template names, Redis's two login variables, and Vault's token; the configuration's KARVI__* variables are read apart, from the process environment, when it loads. The helper may set any portable variable name, and its values reach the credential readers alone, through the lookup: they configure nothing, enter no child's environment, and reach the daemon only inside the sealed credentials. A name it sets overrides the process environment's silently, an override the operator expects; --debug names the path it ran and the names it set, never a value. Three keys, beside creds.interactive-prompt: creds.helper (string, default "karvi-credh"), creds.helper-enabled (boolean, default true), and creds.helper-unavailable (skip, warn, or fail, default skip). A creds.helper without a / is that name in the directory of the running karvi executable, where karvi-askpass is found (the executable a link names, not the link's directory), never searched on PATH; any other value is a place, as every path key is: ~/ under the operator's home, an absolute path as written, a relative path from the working directory (./credh the file in it, since credh alone is beside karvi), each made absolute at load so config show prints the file that runs; ~otheruser is refused as for every place. A site restricts the helper by locking the key. An enabled helper that cannot be run, no file at its path or one the operator cannot execute, is passed by silently under skip, so a site that has placed no karvi-credh sees nothing; under warn a warning names the path and the reason first; under fail the invocation is refused at planning, before any device, credential_helper_unavailable (credential, exit 6), naming the path and the reason. A file present but unsafe is never unavailable (issue 11). A helper that ran and exited non-zero refuses the invocation at planning, credential_helper_failed (credential, exit 6), whatever creds.helper-unavailable says: karvi never proceeds without the values of a helper that failed. --no-credh, accepted by login, command, run (its dry run included), crun, and stream, sets creds.helper-enabled = false for the invocation in the flags layer, as --workers sets its key, the process environment then read as today; a site that locks the key refuses it, config_lock_violation, exit 3. In a stream it is a run option like any other: on the stream's command line it covers every job, as a draft line the jobs whose draft holds it, which neither run nor read the helper. A site that locks creds.helper-enabled to true and creds.helper-unavailable to fail makes its helper mandatory. The name, chosen by the operator from karvi-credh, karvi-credenv, karvi-crede, karvi-plugin, karvi-auth, karvi-env, karvi-cvars, karvi-cv, karvi-ch, karvi-credhelp, and karvi-credhelper: an off-switch should say exactly what it skips, a program run while every other source stays; a name for the variables (credenv, cvars, cv, env) makes --no-… read as "read no credential variables"; auth is the record's word for how a session authenticated, env a backend type, plugin a general word for one narrow role, ch read as the platform's channel, and help a word of karvi's command line (--help, karvi config help), so credhelp reads as help about credentials; the parser's prefix matching makes any long option short to type. Not taken: the credential names alone (the env: cells, the tokens, and the env backend out of the helper's reach); the process environment winning (a stale export defeating the helper); a warning for a shadowed export; a refusal when both set a name; a declared list of the helper's names; PATH searched (a different executable per shell); a restricted path (a second rule beside the place rule every path key takes, where the site's lock is the restriction); ./ required for every path below the working directory (a rule the shell does not have); a required boolean, as credential files have (no warning level); proceeding after a non-zero exit.

Issue 10, the grammar of the helper's output, agreed. Evaluated by dash and bash in an empty environment, the lines a helper might print: inside double quotes a backslash escapes ", \, $, and ` and is kept before anything else ("p\w" is p\w); $word, unquoted or double-quoted, is expanded ("pa$word" is pa), 'pa$word' and "pa\$word" are pa$word; export A=a b drops b; A=a echo INJECTED, export A=x; echo INJECTED, and export A="$(echo INJECTED)" each ran echo; export A=~/x is ~/x to dash and the home to bash. The output is read as text, line by line, and never given to a shell, and a line karvi accepts means what both shells give it under eval, so a site tests its helper with eval "$(karvi-credh)". A blank line is skipped, and so is a comment, its first non-blank character #. Any other line is an optional export and one or more assignments separated by blanks, each a portable variable name, = with no blank on either side (NETPASS = x is refused, a shell running a command NETPASS), and a value: unquoted, of letters, digits, and _ . , : / @ % + = - alone; double-quoted, blanks and ; | & # literal, \", \\, \$, and \` the four escapes, any other backslash kept, and a bare $ or ` refused; single-quoted, every character as written but '; or empty (NETPASS=, "", ''), set and empty. Several assignments on a line are each taken, as a shell takes them; a trailing comment after blanks is ignored, as a shell ignores it. A value holds no control character, a tab and a carriage return included; other bytes are kept as written. Refused, and the run stopped at planning before any device, credential_helper_output_invalid (credential, exit 6), naming the helper's path, the line number, and the reason and quoting nothing from the line (it may be a secret): a blank on either side of =, an unquoted special character, a bare $ or ` in double quotes, a control character, a word that is no assignment (export NETPASS=a b, NETPASS=a echo x), a name assigned twice in the output (both line numbers), and output past 64 KiB or a line past 4 KiB. An empty output is valid. A line karvi cannot read never lets an exported value through in its place: the helper's override (issue 9) holds only for a line it reads. Not taken: evaluating with a shell (the output running commands); the rest of the line taken verbatim (export NETPASS="x" a password with quotes, unlike eval); blanks around = trimmed (not what a shell does); $ expanded from karvi's environment (karvi a shell); ~ unquoted (the shells disagree); the last assignment winning (a script's mistake hidden); a line karvi cannot read dropped silently (a stale export read in its place); a line's text in the error.

Issue 11, the helper file's checks, agreed. A credential file is checked on the descriptor karvi opened, so the file checked is the file read: on the lab build a group-writable CSV was credential_file_mode_unsafe and a symlink to a 0600 one credential_file_symlink_rejected, exit 6, the 0600 file bound; a shared one must be exactly 0640 (0660 and 0600 were credential_file_shared_mode_invalid), its owner reading and writing, the operators' group reading, so that no operator changes the credentials every other operator's runs use. At the operator's question, what ownership and write status risk when the operator may read or run the file anyway: the checks guard against the other accounts of a shared host. On this host, under the shell's umask 0002, a script given +x is 775; a second account in the operator's group, unable to read a stand-in password store (0600), read a 0664 credential CSV directly, and edited a 0775 helper that fetched from the store, the edit replacing the file with one the second account owned through the group-writable directory; the operator ran the helper, its output unchanged, and the second account read the secret it had captured. At 0600 and 0755, in a 0755 directory, it could neither read the CSV, write the helper, nor create a file beside it. A credential file holds secrets, so others reading it is its risk; a helper holds none and runs as the operator with the operator's access, so others changing it is its risk. karvi opens the file creds.helper names, a symlink at the path refused unless security.allow-credential-symlinks is on, as for a credential file, and checks the opened file: a regular file, owned by the operator, root, or a name in security.approved-admin-users, and writable by neither its group nor others (0700, 0750, and 0755 pass, 0775 and 0777 do not); one the operator cannot execute is unavailable (issue 9). The file checked is the file run: karvi runs it through the descriptor it checked, which the build confirms for a script that begins with #!. A failed check is a credential file code, its description widened to the helper and its message naming credential helper PATH: credential_file_symlink_rejected, credential_file_not_regular, credential_file_owner_uninspectable, credential_file_owner_mismatch (owner neither the operator, root, nor an approved administrator), and credential_file_mode_unsafe (writable by group or others; remediation: chmod go-w PATH), each exit 6 whatever creds.helper-unavailable says. The directories on the path are not checked, as for a credential file: a file replaced through a writable directory must still pass the owner and the mode. Not taken: no checks, as git's credential helpers and SSH_ASKPASS have (a site's executable named by a configuration a shared tree carries); the credential file's exact mode (an executable needs x, and 0755 holds no secret); group write allowed under a private group (karvi cannot know the group's members); every directory checked, as OpenSSH checks an AuthorizedKeysCommand (no karvi file takes that rule, and the opened file's owner and mode refuse a substitute); symlinks always followed (a second rule beside the key).

Issue 12, how the helper runs, agreed. karvi's child filter passes a child HOME, USER, LOGNAME, PATH, and the allow-list (LANG, LC_ALL, LC_CTYPE, TERM, COLORTERM, SSH_AUTH_SOCK), and drops the rest of this session's environment, DBUS_SESSION_BUS_ADDRESS (a desktop keyring's client) and XDG_RUNTIME_DIR (where agents keep their sockets) among it; a stand-in helper under set -x, as a site debugs a script, wrote + v=S3cret on standard error, its standard output the one clean line; Ctrl-C at a karvi prompt is exit 113, the code of any cancellation. The helper runs with the operator's environment, karvi's as karvi received it, unfiltered: it is the operator's program, run as if typed in their shell, and fetching a secret needs the session's agents, bus, and client settings; ssh and the daemon keep the filter. It takes no argument; its standard input is /dev/null, so a stream's lines or a piped command file never reach it, and a helper that must ask (a passphrase, a push approval) opens the terminal itself, as pinentry does, before karvi's own prompts and, in a stream, while no line is read; its working directory is the operator's; it runs in karvi's process group, the terminal's foreground one, so it can read the terminal and Ctrl-C reaches it with karvi, the invocation then ending with exit 113 as at any other moment of planning. A new key, creds.helper-timeout (a duration, default 2m, from 1s to 1h), leaves time to type a passphrase or approve a push; past it the helper is sent SIGTERM and, two seconds on, SIGKILL, and the invocation is refused, credential_helper_failed (did not exit within 2m). Its standard error is discarded, never shown or logged, and a failure's message says to run it by hand to see its message. Exit 0 has its output read (issue 10); anything else is credential_helper_failed (exit 6), naming the path and the status or the signal, and a failed helper's output is never read. The values are held as secrets: the raw output overwritten once parsed, each value kept in memory for the invocation, or the stream until --restart, and cleared at its end, never logged, never in --debug (the names alone), and never in a record or the plan but as the credentials the readers build from them, sealed as today. Not taken: the filtered child environment (a helper that unlocks a keyring or reaches an agent failing where it works by hand); an allow-list key of the helper's own (a list learned from its failures); standard input passed through (a stream's next lines consumed); a process group of its own (no terminal to ask at); standard error shown, always or on a failure (a trace printing the secret); no timeout (a helper hung on a dead agent holding the run, and a timer's collection). At the operator's word, every message the helper's path gives, its warning, its refusals, and its notices, is said as karvi says its others at a terminal, in their colours. On the lab build, at a pseudo-terminal, a configuration warning is in the warning colour, a device's failure line during a run in the failure colour, and a refusal at planning, vault_token_missing and every other, in none.

Issue 13, found: refusals without colour at a terminal, agreed. At a pseudo-terminal on the lab build a configuration warning is in the warning colour and a device's failure line during a run in the failure colour, while a refusal at planning (vault_token_missing: 1 of 1 targets failed …) and a stream's dropped line (stream line 1 dropped: cli_option_unknown: …) are in none. Every refusal is printed by one of two functions, reportError (107 call sites) and printResultError (5), and the display has an error role, display.colors.error. A refusal that ends the invocation, the code and its message, is said in the error colour, and a line a stream drops and goes on from in the warning colour, under the display's rules: at a terminal alone, so a redirected stream, a suite, or a log receives no escape; display.color, display.theme, and display.colors.error from the configuration, or the built-in defaults for a refusal before it is read; --nocolor and NO_COLOR turning it off. The helper's warning goes through WriteWarning, as the configuration's warnings do. DISPLAY-CONFIGURATION's colour roles say that refusals take the error role. Not taken: the helper's refusals coloured alone (its errors unlike every other beside them); a role of its own for refusals (the error role means failed); refusals left plain (the line an operator most needs to see).

Issue 14, the helper beside the protocols operators know, agreed. git's credential helper protocol, run with a stand-in helper, writes a request on the helper's standard input (protocol=ssh, host=sw-nyc-01) and reads key=value lines back, the value unquoted to the end of the line, one credential per call for one host; SSH_ASKPASS, which karvi uses through karvi-askpass, gives a program one prompt and reads one answer. karvi's helper takes no request and prints any number of named values once per invocation, as export lines a site tests with eval: its own protocol, issue 10's grammar, with no git or askpass mode, and a site whose secrets sit behind git credential, pass, a keyring, or a vault client writes a karvi-credh that asks them. A helper that asks the operator, a PIN or a push approval, asks on /dev/tty, as pinentry, sudo, and ssh do: run as karvi will run it at a pseudo-terminal, a stand-in that asked on /dev/tty showed its prompt, took the PIN unechoed, and printed the one line karvi read and never displayed; given a wrong PIN it wrote its reason to /dev/tty and exited 1; one that asked on standard error showed nothing, its prompt discarded with the stream, and one that asked on standard output put its prompt into the output, which issue 10 refuses. karvi reports that a helper failed, its path and its exit status, and not why: a reason the operator should see the helper writes to /dev/tty, and with no terminal, under a timer, the documents say to run it by hand. examples/karvi-credh is a commented POSIX sh helper whose core is one quoting function, a value in double quotes with \, ", $, and ` escaped, printing NETUSER, NETPASS, and NETENABLE from pass show, with commented lines for secret-tool lookup, git credential fill, and vault kv get -field, and showing the prompt and a failure's reason on /dev/tty; examples/README.md gives its row and says to install it where it is to run (install -m 0755 examples/karvi-credh /usr/local/lib/karvi/credh, creds.helper naming it), the package never installing a karvi-credh, since the default stays absent until a site places one. A test runs the example's quoting function over hard values (blanks, both quotes, $, \, `, #, ;) and requires karvi's parser and sh -c 'eval …' to read the same values. OPERATIONS gains "The credential helper"; ERROR-CODES, DESIGN, ARCHITECTURE, examples/config.toml, the ROADMAP, and the CHANGELOG follow. Not taken: a git protocol mode (a request per host, where karvi's readers ask by name and one run serves them all); an askpass mode (one prompt, one answer); no example (each site's own quoting, where mistakes are made).

Issue 15, at the operator's word: Redis and Vault answers read once and held, agreed. On the lab build, a dry run of 40 inventory devices made 40 Redis logins and reads (AUTH, HGETALL), or 40 Vault reads, in 1.3 seconds; with Redis at an address that never answers and connect-timeout = "1s", every device waited out its own timeout, 16 at a time, the planning pool's width, and the job was refused after 3.0 seconds, redis_unavailable … 40 of 40 targets failed; at the default timeouts a 2,500-device job against an unreachable store would be refused after about five minutes for Redis (2 seconds each) or thirteen for Vault (5 seconds). The env, Redis, and Vault backends are operator-keyed: their request is the operator's name after the backend's transform, the same for every device, the policy's name stamped on the credential by the resolver after. An operator-keyed backend is asked once per resolver, once per invocation or per job of a stream, and its answer held for every device the policies send to it: a credential, each device given its own copy, as the CSV gives copies of its rows, so that sealing one grant leaves the held answer whole; not found, every device moving on without asking again; a failure, every device given it without asking again, a dead store costing one timeout. Devices resolving in parallel wait for the one read, as credfile.Cache holds a CSV's rows; a formula over Redis or Vault takes its source's held answer; the credentials, matched_on, and merged grants are as before. A stream asks per job, as a CSV is read per job, so a password rotated in the store reaches the next job without --restart, the helper's values alone held across jobs (issue 8), since only the helper may ask the operator. Tests: a counting Redis and Vault give one read for many devices, and an unanswering store is asked once. Not taken: a cache across invocations (secrets at rest or in a resident process); store answers held across a stream's jobs (a rotation waiting for --restart); failures not held (a dead store holding a large job for minutes); planning stopped at the first failure (a change to the planner, where the held failure gives one timeout).

Issue 16, at the operator's word: TLS to Redis and Vault, agreed. On the lab build, against TLS stores, with a site CA and another CA standing in for one the host trusts (Go's system pool given it by SSL_CERT_FILE): a server from the site CA without ca-file was refused, x509: certificate signed by unknown authority, nothing sent, and with ca-file naming the site CA read; a server from the other CA, ca-file naming the site CA, was read while the host trusted the other CA, the token sent to a server the named CA never vouched for, and refused while it did not; a store requiring a client certificate refused a client without one (tls: certificate required) and took client-cert-file and client-key-file, the key at mode 0644 read unchecked; Redis, under TLS by default, the same. The server's name is checked against the address and TLS 1.2 is the least, and no key skips verification. A new key, tls-verify (boolean, default true), on Redis and Vault backends: true verifies the server's certificate chain and its name, the chain leading to a CA in ca-file when it is given, the file then the backend's whole trust, or to the host's trust (the public roots and the CAs the site installed) when it is not; false checks neither, the connection still encrypted. A self-signed store is verified by naming its own certificate in ca-file: on the lab a self-signed Vault was refused without it and read with it. ca-file under tls-verify = false, and tls-verify on Redis under tls = false or on Vault with an http:// address, are keys left unread, refused at load (issue 3). Mutual TLS stays optional: given client-cert-file and client-key-file, a pair, karvi presents the certificate and every further certificate in the file as its chain, and checks neither file's permissions, since one certificate may serve a site's machine authentication, a store may be shared or run by a service account on a host of its own, and restricting the files is the site's choice as it installs them; a file karvi cannot read fails as today (redis_tls_config_invalid, vault_tls). Plain text, Redis's tls = false or a Vault http:// address, and tls-verify = false are the site's choices, without a warning. OPERATIONS's Redis and Vault sections say what each setting trusts, that without verification a host on the path can pose as the store and receive its token or password, and how a site trusts a self-signed store: best, the certificate file copied from the store's host; else collected with openssl s_client -connect HOST:PORT </dev/null 2>/dev/null | openssl x509 -out FILE, which on the lab took the self-signed Vault's certificate and a TLS Redis's alike, its SHA-256 fingerprint compared with the one read on the store's host, then named in ca-file with tls-verify left true, a dry run reading the store; a replaced certificate is refused until ca-file holds the new one; a store under a private CA is trusted through that CA's certificate; the file is public, any mode. examples/config.toml's commented Vault block carries the same lines. Not taken: ca-file added to the host's trust, as today (any CA the host trusts vouching for the store); no way to skip verification; a warning under tls-verify = false or plain text; a permission check on the client key (a site's machine credential as often as a user's).

Issue 17, found while building S1: an empty value, agreed. On the lab builds of 9b722da and of S1, a Vault backend whose mount, path-template, namespace, and three field names were each "" validated and read /v1/secret/data/karvi/netops, every one silently its default; Redis's key-template = "" read karvi:credential:netops; and Redis's ca-file and client pair at "" validated, the run trusting the host's CAs and presenting no certificate. At the operator's questions, on the lab build of S1: a setting is left undefined by leaving its key out, TOML having no null, a fixed key then taking its built-in default (config show's source <builtin>) and a backend's key the default its backend gives it, a value (Vault's mount, secret) or none (Vault's namespace); with no configuration file karvi runs on its built-in defaults, with no backend table, creds.helper is karvi-credh, passed by silently under skip when absent, so no default gives "" and an empty value is always written; auto is karvi's word where it computes a place from a chain, none is sharedroot's for consulting nothing, default names a table, a policy, an order, or a colour, and "" is already none where a key's default is none (audit.file, a display template); a default is never judged, a Redis backend under tls = false or a Vault one at an http:// address with nothing else written being valid; and a ca-file naming a file without TLS is refused (issue 3). Layers merge a backend's table key by key: a second file set namespace = "" and ca-file = "" over a first file's values and could not remove either key, so "" is the only way a later layer, or --set, says none over an earlier one's value, where a key whose default is a value restores it by writing it (mount = "secret"). A key whose default is none takes "" as that none, the same as the key left out and never a key left unread (issue 3): the env backend's three templates; Redis's ca-file, client-cert-file, client-key-file, auth-username-env, and auth-password-env; and Vault's namespace, ca-file, and client pair. ca-file = "" under tls = false passes, and a client certificate beside client-key-file = "" is refused for want of its key. This amends issue 4, which refused "" for auth-username-env, auth-password-env, and the env templates. A key whose default is a value refuses "", its own value spelling the default: Vault's mount, path-template, username-field, password-field, and enable-field, Redis's key-template, transform on the three types, and creds.helper, whose off switch is creds.helper-enabled = false; the refusal is config_type_error (exit 2), must not be empty, when the configuration loads; token-env keeps issue 4's code and address its own. A written value, the default's included, is a setting like any other, judged by issues 3 and 16: tls-verify = true under tls = false or with an http:// address stays refused, at the operator's word, the written true claiming a verification the plain connection never makes, where left out it claims nothing. Not taken: a word none (a new spelling, and one a value may hold); "" refused on every key (a later layer unable to undo an earlier one's value); "" as the default where the default is a value (two spellings); a written default never refused (the refusal kept on tls-verify = false, which claims nothing untrue, and lifted from true, which does).

Issue 18, found while building S3: what a helper leaves running, agreed. At the operator's questions, on the lab build of S3: a helper that prints and exits at once costs its own time (2 ms), its output read to the end of the pipe, which closes as it exits; the two seconds after an exit are spent only when a child it left in the background holds its output (2.004 s); a compiled Go program at mode 0711, which the operator may execute and not read, runs as a script does; an exit 0 with no output sets nothing, every name the environment's. A helper that never exits was refused after creds.helper-timeout, credential_helper_failed, two seconds later if it ignored SIGTERM, and left running the processes it had started: a fetch hung in a command substitution (v=$(…), the shape of examples/karvi-credh's pass show), which SIGTERM to the helper's shell never reached; a child in the background; a child that ignored SIGTERM as its helper did and held its output, so karvi waited its two seconds for the end, 7 seconds at a 3-second timeout; and the same fetch when karvi was sent SIGTERM, as a timer's unit is stopped, exit 113. Without a terminal each stays running after karvi ends, one more for each timed-out run of an unattended collection. The helper runs in karvi's process group (issue 12), so karvi cannot signal the group without signalling itself. A prototype ran a helper both ways at a pseudo-terminal: in a group of its own, made the terminal's foreground while it runs, a timeout's signal to the group ended every child and a PIN asked on /dev/tty was read, but Ctrl-C reached the helper alone, one that caught it carrying on and karvi going on with its output, and Ctrl-Z stopped the helper alone, the shell given the terminal back only at the timeout; in karvi's group Ctrl-C reached both and Ctrl-Z stopped both, fg resuming them. The helper stays in karvi's process group. When it is to be ended, past creds.helper-timeout or with the invocation cancelled, karvi lists the processes descended from it, by their parents in /proc, while it still runs, and sends SIGTERM to the helper and each of them, and two seconds on SIGKILL to each still running, listing them again first while the helper runs; each is held by a pidfd opened while its start time matched the listing, so no signal reaches a process that took its pid since. A process that has left the tree, its parent ended before it (a daemon's double fork, as gpg-agent makes), is no descendant and is left running, and a helper that exits by itself, 0 or not, leaves what it started, as it chose. On the lab build of issue 18 nothing was left running in those cases but the double fork's, the stubborn child's took 5 seconds, and the cancelled run's fetch ended with it. creds.helper-timeout's default is 60s, at the operator's word, amending issue 12's 2m: a helper that never exits is refused 60 seconds from its start, 62 if it ignores SIGTERM, 64 at most if a process outside its tree holds its output. Not taken: a group of its own (karvi's Ctrl-C lost to a helper that catches it, and Ctrl-Z leaving the terminal held); a group of its own without a terminal alone (two behaviours, a terminal's strays kept); the strays left as the helper's own (a timer's run adding one each time); karvi made the child subreaper, adopting the orphans (every orphan below karvi, an ssh control master's among them, karvi's to reap); descendants ended after a helper exits by itself (an agent it started on purpose ended).
