57. A build names its tree (2026-10-12) #

The ROADMAP's Next item 1, opened as build numbers and rescoped at the operator's word (deac902): two builds of different trees told apart wherever a build is named; the daemon's part buildable now, the stamp beside the next release.

The evidence. What each build says in karvi version, which reads no configuration:

Build commit build_time
the released bin/ source-release-v0.29.0 2026-10-09T00:00:00Z
the dev line's make build of 70d6f5c development 1970-01-01T00:00:00Z
the same of be2d8d7 development 1970-01-01T00:00:00Z
a lab build of be2d8d7 be2d8d7-lab the second it was built
a release-style make build of be2d8d7, twice source-release-v0.29.0 the day's midnight

The two dev-line builds are different programs and say the same, in karvi version and in a job's manifest; the two release-style builds were byte for byte the same. A job's manifest names the build that ran it (app.build) and the client's version and commit (header.client); daemon status, an audit event's process, and the scoreboard's producer name the version alone. The be2d8d7 build's client asking the 70d6f5c build's daemon was told version: 0.29.0 and compatible: true, nothing naming the build.

What it gains. In development, a build that names its tree and a daemon that names its build: chapter 56's lab stopped the daemon between builds for want of it. For a site on releases, nothing: a release is one identity, its rebuild byte-identical.

What it waits on. The stamp waits on the next release, whose tools set and check it (the day's midnight, the source-release-v commit, scripts/verify-release.sh, scripts/build-deb.sh); the daemon's part waits on nothing. Rescoped at the operator's word from a build counter, which nothing would read and which a release's one identity would break, to make build's default commit taken from git and the daemon's commit named in daemon status and the compatibility line.

The operator's rule: a daemon of another version or build, agreed. On a lab of three builds, A a lab build of deac902, B the same tree stamped otherwise, and V the same tree at version 0.29.9, with A's daemon running a 20-second job on the fake device: B's daemon status said compatible: true, and B's run went to A's daemon, its manifest naming A's build and B's client; V's run was refused, daemon_incompatible (exit 112), the daemon not replaced; V's daemon restart was refused while the job ran, and --grace waited without draining a daemon of another version, so a run of A's client during the wait was accepted before V stopped the daemon and started its own. Three ways to have a daemon finish its jobs and exit exist. SIGTERM drained it at once (a new run daemon_draining, exit 112) and it exited at its job's end, 20.4 s; but at daemon.shutdown-grace-seconds (120 by default) it cancels what still runs, and at 5 the job ended incomplete_shutdown, exit 106. A drain sent as V's client would send it was accepted, the daemon checking the IPC schema alone, and the job finished; but the daemon stayed, draining with no job. The client's --grace waits in the client. Under a user unit with the packaged unit's Restart=on-failure, an install that replaced the executable left the daemon running the old program; the installed client's daemon restart stopped it, exit 0, which systemd did not restart, and started its own in the operator's session scope, outside the unit; systemctl --user restart started the installed build in the unit. The rule. A daemon serves a client only when its version, its IPC schema, and its build all equal the client's. A client that reaches a daemon of another version or build drains it: from then the daemon admits no job from any client, finishes every job it holds with no time limit, and exits after the last, killing nothing. A daemon holding no job exits at once, and the client starts a daemon of its own build and runs its job in the same invocation; for one holding jobs, the client refuses its job, naming the daemon's version, its build, the jobs it is finishing, and --no-daemon, and every client's job is refused alike until the daemon has exited, the first client after it starting a daemon of its own build. The drain is the existing request, its daemon now exiting after its last job: a drained daemon with no job refuses every job and serves nothing, and --grace and --after, the drain's only senders, stop it next. The drain joins the lifecycle requests sent at the daemon's own schema, as stop is, so it reaches a daemon of an older schema. Two builds used in turn drain each other's daemon, and a second build's way is --no-daemon; a binary elsewhere on the host beside the installed one is the operator's to sort out. This replaces DESIGN section 8's entry "A client keeps lifecycle access to a daemon of another release, and compatibility is the pair", whose Why said a client never kills an incompatible daemon automatically and whose Not taken named comparing the commit. Not taken: the signal (it cancels the jobs past the old daemon's grace, and lifting that lengthens a systemctl stop); a new request beside the drain (the same effect, keeping a drain without an exit that nothing uses); the client waiting out the drain (a run held for the old jobs' length, and an interrupt leaving the daemon draining); a second daemon beside the draining one (two daemons for one operator, the old jobs out of reach of job follow and job cancel, the job and capacity limits counted twice); a notice alone, as the ROADMAP had it.

Its issues, one at a time: the build's identity, what is compared; the daemon's answer naming its build, and whether it moves the IPC schema; found while designing that answer, what the client reports to the daemon and the moment between its probe and its submission; the drain that exits; the client's sequence, its refusal, and the commands that apply the rule; a draining daemon's jobs reached from another build; the daemon under a systemd unit; the stamp and the release tools' checks; and the documents.

Issue 1, the build's identity, agreed. In a worktree of deac902, make build with its commit taken from git describe --always --dirty: the clean tree said karvi-v0.29.0-43-gdeac902, naming the tag before it; after one string changed, and after a second, both said karvi-v0.29.0-43-gdeac902-dirty, two programs whose executables and Go build IDs differed; a comment added to the second changed neither its executable nor its build ID; two release-style builds were the same in both, and the lab builds A and B, which differ by their stamp alone, differed in both. Go's own stamp, -buildvcs=true, records vcs.modified=true, the same for every change. The build ID, actionID(binary)/actionID(main.a)/contentID(main.a)/contentID(binary) in the executable's ELF note .note.go.buildid, was read from a 12 MB executable in 7 to 11 µs, where its SHA-256 took 32 to 34 ms; a running program whose file had been deleted gave its own through /proc/PID/exe. The rule. A build is derived from the Go build ID its executable carries, which the process reads from its own executable, /proc/self/exe: the daemon at its start, the client when it reaches a daemon. After the IPC schema, which must match for the daemon's answer to be read, the build decides: a daemon is of the client's build when its build equals the client's, and the version and the commit, compiled into the program, differ only with it; a daemon that names no build is of another build. The version and the commit stay what an operator reads, in daemon status and in the refusal; the commit taken from git names the tree for people and no longer has to tell two programs apart. Not taken: the commit alone (two programs of a modified tree, one name); Go's VCS stamp (the same, and it needs .git); the commit with the modified tree's contents hashed (a comment another build, only make build stamping it, and two toolchains or flags on one tree one name); the executable's SHA-256 (the same verdicts, at 33 ms an invocation).

Issue 2, the daemon's answer and where a build is named, agreed. Go's build ID for an executable is four hashes, actionID(binary)/actionID(main.a)/contentID(main.a)/contentID(binary), the last "a hash of the action output, namely the archive or binary itself" (cmd/go/internal/work/buildid.go), stored so that it is read without hashing the file: the last parts of issue 1's builds were Fjp47FjoGz2mPlGLdc4E (the first change), XfTtoeK1qq9HkGakcVu7 (the second, and the comment), and koTfnKBP37SejCB-tkZv (both release-style builds). A build made with -ldflags=-buildid= carries none, and runs. The ping and status answer is the response's result, which the IPC schema leaves unconstrained. The rule. The build is the build ID's last part, its 20 characters shown as they are. The ping and status answer gains build and commit, the client comparing the IPC schema and then the build, and no schema moves. daemon status names the daemon's values that have a client's beside them with daemon_, as the client's carry client_, and stacks each pair, the daemon's over the client's, keys of one length so that the values line up: daemon_version: over client_version:, daemon_commit: over client_commit:, daemon_ipc_schema: over client_ipc_schema:, and daemon_build: over client_build:, the last pair over compatible:, which the schema and the build decide; the lines without a client's value keep their names. When colour is on, each pair's two values are in the success colour when they are equal and in the error colour when not, and compatible:'s value alike. Its JSON takes the same keys (amended at the operator's word, the daemon's version once version: and its schema ipc_schema_version there). Beside a daemon of another build it reads:

status: running
pid: 2267268
uid: 1000
socket: /home/netops/.local/share/karvi/socket/d.sock
daemon_version: 0.29.0
client_version: 0.29.0
daemon_commit: karvi-v0.29.0-43-gdeac902
client_commit: karvi-v0.29.0-43-gdeac902-dirty
daemon_ipc_schema: 11
client_ipc_schema: 11
daemon_build: 9Au3WHU839F5xE-67H93
client_build: XfTtoeK1qq9HkGakcVu7
compatible: false
remediation: karvi daemon restart
started_at: 2026-10-12T01:34:55.610075038-04:00
active_jobs: 1
accepted_jobs: 3

karvi version gains build: after commit:, and with it the manifest's app.build, the same identity under an open schema, names the build that ran each job. A process that cannot read its own build stops: daemon serve refuses to start, and a client refuses to submit through a daemon, each with a registered code, build_unreadable; an empty build is never compared. --no-daemon and karvi version, which says build: none, are unaffected. Not taken: the builds four lines apart, each after its version (two values compared are read at a glance stacked); the whole build ID (83 characters, four hashes, the program the last); a hash of karvi's own (a second name for what Go names); buildid as the field (Go's name for the whole string); the build in every record, an audit event's process and the scoreboard's producer (the manifest names it once a job, and every record names its job).

Issue 3, found while designing the daemon's answer: the client controls, agreed. Every request carries client_version and client_pid, which the schema requires and the daemon reads neither of; the job header's client (version, commit, pid, hostname) is read by nothing, only recorded in the manifest; and the client probes the daemon and then sends prepare_job on a new connection, nothing holding the daemon between the two, so a daemon replaced in that moment takes the job, as a daemon took another build's job on the lab. The daemon's answer to prepare_job already reports the preparing daemon, its daemon block holding pid, version, and ipc_schema_version under an open schema, read today by the exercise report and the dry run alone. The rule. Whether a daemon is acceptable is the client's judgment alone: the daemon takes a job from a client of any version or build and checks nothing of the client's, so a later client may accept a daemon of another version or build without the daemon changing. The client reports neither its version nor its build to the daemon: the request loses client_version, and client_pid, which nothing reads either; the job header's client keeps the client's pid and hostname and loses its version and commit. The daemon reports its own, and the client closes the moment between its probe and its submission: the prepare answer's daemon block gains build and commit, as the status does, and a client that finds the preparing daemon unacceptable abandons the preparation before any credential is sent, the preparation expiring in the daemon's sweep, and begins its sequence again. The request moves the IPC schema to 12, and the header the execution plan's to 13 with the planner's pinned draft digest; the daemon block's fields move nothing. A manifest no longer names the build of the client that planned its job, under this rule always app.build's; a client that accepts another daemon would bring it back. Not taken: the daemon checking the client's build (the judgment is the client's); the moment left open (a daemon replaced between the probe and the request takes the job).

Issue 4, the drain that exits, agreed. On the lab build, a daemon drained during a 20-second job served its run to the end and a second follower, job follow from the same build, both receiving the terminal at 20.4 s, and then stayed, draining with no job, until stopped; during the drain daemon stop was refused, daemon_active_jobs (exit 112), and --force cut the job, incomplete_shutdown (exit 106); daemon stop --grace drained, polled until no job was active, and stopped, the job ending at 20.4 s and the daemon gone at 20.6 s. In the code, the commit of a preparation made before a drain is refused, daemon_draining, and a follow stream under the server's cancel waits for its job and sends the terminal. The rule. A drained daemon exits when its last running job ends, and at once with none: no time limit, nothing killed, the drain answered first, stopping: drained logged, exit 0. Only accepted jobs hold it; a preparation not yet committed does not, its commit refused. Until it exits it serves as before: ping and status (draining, its jobs), follow_job and cancel_job for its jobs, every follower receiving its terminal before the exit; prepare_job and commit_job refused, daemon_draining; a stop during a drain as at any other time, refused while jobs run, --force cancelling them, a signal applying its grace; a second drain answered draining. The drain is sent at the daemon's own IPC schema, as stop is, so it reaches a daemon of an older schema. daemon stop --grace becomes a drain and a wait for the daemon to go, --after=DURATION a drain, a wait up to the limit, and a forced stop, and daemon restart with either the same and then a start; an interrupt ends the wait alone, the daemon finishing its jobs and exiting, as its message says; and both drain whatever daemon they reach, where --grace did not drain a daemon of another version. A client replaces an idle daemon by draining it, waiting up to daemon.start-timeout for it to go, and starting its own. Not taken: a time limit (the jobs finish); a drained daemon kept after its last job (it refuses every job); the draining daemon starting its successor (the client starts one, or the unit).

Issue 5, the client's sequence, its refusal, and the commands, agreed. On the lab, beside an idle daemon of another version, daemon start, run --exercise, run --detach, and each job of a stream were refused, daemon_incompatible (exit 112), the stream going on to its next job; the dry run reported the daemon incompatible with the error finding "a live run would not replace it", exit 112; beside one of another build of the same version, each used it. Every submission, run, crun, --exercise, --detach, and a stream's job, reaches the daemon through one function, ensureDaemon, as do daemon start and daemon restart after its stop; RunViaDaemon already repeats its first request once when the daemon is gone or draining. The rule. Before every submission the client probes: nothing answering, it starts a daemon of its build; its own build running, it uses it; another build, or any daemon draining, it drains it at the daemon's own schema and reads its jobs from the answer. With none the daemon exits at once, and the client waits up to daemon.start-timeout for it to go, starts its own, and goes on, saying daemon drained version=… build=… before daemon started socket=… unless --quiet, the notice without the commit as the refusal is (at the operator's word); with jobs, or not gone within the wait (the refusal then naming karvi daemon stop), the submission is refused, daemon_draining, exit 112, the daemon's build over the client's, each with its version and no commit, and every line in the refusal's colour when colour is on, opened and closed on the line itself, by the display's one rule (plain under --nocolor, through a pipe unless display.color = always, and in a file):

daemon_draining: 1 job running; none submitted; retry when it exits, or --no-daemon
  daemon build 9Au3WHU839F5xE-67H93 0.29.0
  client build XfTtoeK1qq9HkGakcVu7 0.29.0

A daemon whose schema the client cannot speak, a newer one, is refused daemon_incompatible as before, its text naming karvi daemon stop from the newer karvi and no longer saying it was not replaced automatically. A prepare answer naming another build (issue 3) abandons the preparation and runs the sequence once more, within the repeat that exists; a second is refused alike. run, crun, --exercise, --detach, every job of a stream (a refused job reported and the stream going on), and daemon start drain; daemon stop and daemon restart are explicit and keep issue 4's options; daemon status reads and never drains; --no-daemon never reaches the daemon. The dry run says what a live run would do and drains nothing: for an idle daemon of another build an info finding, daemon_other_build, a live run draining it and starting one of this build; for one with jobs the error finding daemon_draining, exit 112. Not taken: the client waiting out the jobs (the rule refuses); a code of its own for the refusal (daemon_draining already says the daemon takes no job, whoever drained it); an older client draining a newer daemon (the daemon would answer every older schema; after an upgrade an older karvi's operator stops it explicitly); the builds on one line (the two set one over the other are read at a glance); the commit beside them (the build tells them apart).

Issue 6, a daemon's jobs reached from another build, agreed. On the lab, beside a daemon running a job, a client of another version was refused job follow as job_orphaned, "no running daemon holds the job", while that daemon ran it, the job directory's state judged before the daemon's, and job cancel as daemon_incompatible (exit 112); after the job's end its follow rendered from the files, exit 0. A client of another build of the same version cancelled the job, and its follow, begun before, showed the cancel, exit 113. The follow stream and the cancel are one wire under one IPC schema, and a follower decodes each record as it reads a finished job's files, which any build reads. The rule. job follow and job cancel use a daemon of any version or build that speaks the client's IPC schema, the client's judgment (issue 3) for verbs that plan nothing: after an upgrade the jobs a draining daemon finishes are followed and cancelled one at a time, not only all at once with daemon stop --force; neither drains, since neither submits. A daemon whose schema the client cannot speak refuses a running job's follow and cancel, daemon_incompatible, naming the karvi that speaks it, and a job that has ended still renders from its directory. Found: such a job is never reported job_orphaned, the daemon judged before the directory. The probe answers two questions: a submission needs the schema and the build, follow and cancel the schema. Not taken: follow and cancel refused across builds (the old daemon's jobs out of reach but by cancelling them all); a drain on follow or cancel (nothing is submitted).

Issue 7, the daemon under a systemd unit, agreed. On transient user units with the packaged unit's restart settings: an install that replaced the executable left the unit's daemon running the old program; drained or stopped, it exits 0, which Restart=on-failure does not restart, and the next client started one in the operator's session, outside the unit. The unit's daemon carries INVOCATION_ID and SYSTEMD_EXEC_PID and the unit's cgroup, a client-launched daemon neither. A client run 0.3 s after the unit's daemon was killed started its own, and the unit's restarts failed, daemon_already_running (exit 1), five in 12 s. RestartForceExitStatus= restarts an exit it names, and a socket unit started a service that had exited 0 at the next connection. An operator of another UID was refused at the socket's directory (drwx------), Permission denied, before a byte, the daemon's peer check behind it. systemd's stop timeout defaults to 90 s beside the daemon's 120-second grace: with 5 s standing for it, systemctl stop during a 20-second job killed the draining daemon at 5.3 s, the run losing its follow (follow_stream_lost, exit 111) and the job its summary; with TimeoutStopSec=infinity the stop waited for the job, which ended exit 0 with its summary. The rule. Two shapes: an operator's own daemon, launched by the operator's client under the operator's UID, as on a jump box, under issues 1 to 6; and a service account's daemon under its systemd unit, centrally administered, whose life is the unit's and its administrator's, which a client never drains, replaces, or starts a daemon beside. Only the daemon's own UID reaches it, so an individual operator sends no job to a service account's daemon. A daemon started by systemd knows it from INVOCATION_ID at its start and names its unit in daemon status, unit: karvi-daemon.service, text and JSON; a client-launched daemon names none. A unit's daemon of another build is refused, never drained, in issue 5's layout:

daemon_incompatible: karvi-daemon.service runs another build; none submitted; restart the unit, or --no-daemon
  daemon build 9Au3WHU839F5xE-67H93 0.29.0
  client build XfTtoeK1qq9HkGakcVu7 0.29.0

A new key, daemon.launch (default true), set false in the service account's or the site's configuration and lockable, keeps every client from starting a daemon: one that finds none refuses, daemon_unreachable, the daemon being started by its unit, and daemon start refuses alike. karvi daemon stop and daemon restart refuse a unit's daemon, naming systemctl --user stop or restart of its unit. The packaged unit gains TimeoutStopSec=infinity, the daemon's grace keys bounding its stop, so a site that raises the grace changes no unit. After an install the administrator restarts the unit, which drains the old daemon under its grace and starts the installed build; OPERATIONS says so as the unit's part of an upgrade, and the idle timer stays off under the unit. daemon.launch is documented as the key of the centralized install, a service account's daemon under its unit, which packagers and sites are to note (at the operator's word): OPERATIONS gains that install method, the service account, its user manager kept running (loginctl enable-linger), the packaged user unit installed for it, daemon.launch = false in its configuration or, for every operator of the host, locked in the site's global file, and the administrator's restart after an install; the packaged unit's comments name the key and the restart; examples/config.toml carries the key, commented, with its reason; FILES section 4.7 (what the package installs) and BUILD-HOWTO section 4.3 (the Debian package) point to the method; and the reference, the manual page karvi-daemon(1), and the CHANGELOG name it. In daemon status, unit: follows socket:, and a unit's daemon's remediation: names systemctl --user restart of its unit. Not taken: socket activation, and an exit status of its own for systemd to restart (a client's touch choosing the moment the service changes build, which is the administrator's); Restart=always (a stop undone, and the moment before a restart left open); a client draining a unit's daemon (the administrator's act).

Issue 8, the stamp and the release tools' checks; first, the stamp, agreed. The documents' command that reproduces a release's executables, make build COMMIT=source-release-v$(cat VERSION) BUILD_TIME=2026-09-30T00:00:00Z in the README, BUILDING, BUILD-HOWTO section 4.2, and BUILD-QUALIFICATION, was run on the v0.29.0 bundle: all three executables failed sha256sum -c CHECKSUMS.sha256; with the release's own date, 2026-10-09, all three passed, the build the shipped one's, gyzw8oOlJCNN-GsQ0BRT. That date was right for v0.24.0 and v0.25.0 alone, and in the bundle the release's date is written only in the CHANGELOG's heading and release/evidence/version.txt. The tree deac902 built twice with the time it was built, two seconds apart, gave two builds, WErTs2nB46MMqFsVvCKo and I13-a3nJoa99NEpnQTvN, and twice with one fixed time one build and the same bytes. A dev build had two defaults, the Makefile's development and 1970 and verify-release.sh's own git rev-parse --short=12 HEAD and the time it ran. git describe --always --dirty named a clean clone of dev karvi-v0.29.0-43-gdeac902, added -dirty while a tracked file differed and not for an untracked one, and with no tag reachable gave the commit's hash where --dirty alone failed; in a copy without .git placed inside another checkout it named that checkout's commit. The rule. A build is stamped with its version and its commit, and the build time is removed: from karvi version, text and JSON, and with it the manifest's app.build; from the Makefile and verify-release.sh; and from the release tools, common.sh's midnight and evidence-rest.sh's two passes. Nothing compares it; a release's repeats the CHANGELOG's heading; a dev build's says nothing at 1970 and makes every make build another build at the time it ran; and it is the one value a rebuild must be told from outside the tree. karvi version reads:

karvi 0.29.0
commit: karvi-v0.29.0-43-gdeac902-dirty
build: XfTtoeK1qq9HkGakcVu7
go: go1.27.1

make build's commit is git describe --always --dirty in a git checkout's root, .git present there, and development elsewhere. It is the one default: verify-release.sh loses its own, make taking COMMIT from the environment when it is set, and make html's footer takes the same. A release passes COMMIT=source-release-vVERSION, as its tools do, and make build COMMIT=source-release-v$(cat VERSION) reproduces its bytes, the command the documents give. verify-shipped.sh and verify-release.sh check that karvi version names a build, 20 characters and never none, so that a release without its Go build ID, whose daemon would refuse to start, is found in seconds; build-deb.sh's check that a dev-line package carries no release's commit stays; and at the next release BUILD-RESULT.md's identity block and release-manifest.json name the build where they named the build time. An untracked file leaves the commit clean: the build tells such builds apart (issue 1), and a release tree holds none (release-tools/artifacts.sh). Not taken: the dates derived, a release's from the CHANGELOG's heading and a dev build's from its commit (dates the version and the commit imply, and a modified tree dated by a commit it does not match); the release's commit derived from the heading (a modified tree under it would claim the release); the tag's name, which git describe gives at the tag (the bundle has no .git, so its rebuild would say development).

Issue 8, continued: the lifecycle replay, agreed. The next release moves the IPC schema to 12, so the newest release of another schema is v0.29.0. Its daemon, idle and drained at schema 11 as the new client drains one, was still there 6.3 s later, draining with no job, and a run was refused daemon_draining (exit 112); its own client's daemon stop --grace, which waits for no job and then stops, ended it in 0.1 s. Under issues 4 and 5 a replay against it would wait without end on --grace and refuse its run after daemon.start-timeout. run --exercise through a daemon to 192.0.2.1 with the replay's credentials exited 0, device_contacted: false, no ssh process started. The rule. Nothing is done for a daemon from before this chapter (at the operator's word: no one runs one, and the next client never reaches its daemon.sock), and issues 4 and 5 stand. The next release has no lifecycle replay: at that release common.sh's PRIOR_VERSION is emptied, its comment saying why, v0.29.0's daemon predating the drain that exits and listening on daemon.sock; evidence-rest.sh's skip line names common.sh for the reason, no longer saying that no release's schema differs; and BUILD-QUALIFICATION's history of priors says there was none for it. In the build the replay moves to this rule: the status read by its stacked keys; its run step run --exercise to 192.0.2.1, the idle older daemon drained, exiting, and replaced by the client's; and --grace and --after draining first. Its first run is at the release that next moves the schema, against the next release. Not taken: the wait after a drain ending at no job and stopping a daemon still answering, and a daemon's missing commit and build shown none (each for a past release's daemon alone); the replay run in the build against a lab build at another schema.

Issue 9, the documents, agreed. The documents that say what this chapter changes, found by their words (compatible, daemon restart, --grace, drain, daemon_incompatible, build_time, Restart=, upgrad): DESIGN sections 7, 8, and 16; OPERATIONS' "Upgrade" and "The daemon's idle exit"; QUICKSTART's "After upgrading from an older daemon"; ARCHITECTURE's "Daemon upgrade boundary"; README's "Release status"; BUILD-HOWTO sections 4.2, 4.3, and 8; BUILDING; BUILD-QUALIFICATION; the manual page karvi-daemon(1), its DESCRIPTION generated from the help and its FILES written by hand; ERROR-CODES and the reference, generated from their registries; examples/config.toml; the packaged unit; FILES section 4.7; the CHANGELOG; and the ROADMAP. The rule. The documents say what this chapter builds, and nothing of a daemon from before it. The generated ones move with their code in each section of the build, make generated-clean holding at every commit: ERROR-CODES (build_unreadable and daemon_other_build new; daemon_incompatible a newer schema, or a unit's daemon of another build; daemon_draining the client's own drain of a busy daemon as well; daemon_stop_wait_interrupted the daemon finishing its jobs and exiting, no longer remaining draining); the reference and the configuration schema (daemon.launch, its documentation naming the centralized install); and karvi-daemon(1)'s SYNOPSIS and DESCRIPTION from the help.

The written ones come in the build's closing section, one commit, then make html. Issue 10, found while building S3: the stop's mode, its count, and the wait, agreed. On the lab build of section 3, during a 30-second job, a stop naming if_idle was refused, daemon_active_jobs, and one naming no mode was answered stopping: the run lost its follow at 5.1 s (follow_stream_lost, exit 111), and the daemon left at 10.1 s logging shutdown_incomplete, the job's folder without its summary. The client named the mode only to a daemon it judged compatible, of its schema and version and, since section 1, its build, so a build of section 2 beside the section's daemon sent daemon stop --force with none: daemon_stop_timeout (exit 106) after 10 s and the job lost so, where a build of section 3 had it recorded incomplete_shutdown (exit 106) with its summary. With no option that client counted the daemon's jobs itself, from its probe, which no job another client submits before the stop reaches. A drained daemon stopped with --force logged stopping: drained as its cancelled job ended, and one sent SIGTERM after a drain logged the signal's stop and the drain's. --grace's client waited without a word; ended by SIGINT or SIGTERM (exit 113, its message), SIGHUP (129), or SIGKILL (137), it left the drained daemon to finish its job and exit; and an --after=5s interrupted at 1 s forced nothing at 5 s. The rule. Every stop and restart the client sends names its mode, if_idle without an option and force under --force and at --after's limit, and the client counts nothing: the daemon alone counts its jobs, under the lock that admits them, so that no job is admitted between the count and the stop; it refuses an if_idle stop while any run and takes a stop that names no mode as if_idle; --grace, --after, and --force overrule the refusal (at the operator's word, no daemon of an older release considered). A stop or a signal after a drain takes over from it, so that the log names the stop that ended the daemon. A --grace or --after wait on a daemon with jobs begins with a line on standard error, the jobs from the drain's answer and an earlier drain from the probe's status, every time, so that an operator back after a broken wait is told; --quiet leaves it out:

daemon draining: 1 job running; waiting for it to exit
daemon already draining: 2 jobs running; waiting for it to exit
daemon draining: 1 job running; waiting up to 5s, then forcing

Not taken: the client's count before the stop (blind to other clients, the daemon's needed anyway); a stop naming no mode stopping at once (its jobs lost unrecorded); the daemon holding --after's limit, so that it outlives the client (at the operator's word, the wait and its limit are the client's).

Issue 11, found while building S3: the grace and systemd's stop limits, agreed. This host's systemd 259 (Ubuntu) stops a login session's scope, where a client-launched daemon runs, in 90 s, KillMode=control-group, and user@.service, the user manager under which a user unit runs, in 5 s, by the package's drop-in user@.service.d/timeout.conf, KillMode=mixed; read from the configuration, no shutdown run. At a shutdown a daemon receives SIGTERM, drains, every new job refused (daemon_draining), and waits daemon.shutdown-grace-seconds, 120 by default, for its jobs before cancelling and recording them: the scope's 90 s killed it first. Scaled on transient user scopes with TimeoutStopSec=5, a 30-second job stopped with the scope: under a grace of 3 the job was recorded incomplete (exit 106) with its summary and the socket removed; under a grace of 8 the daemon was killed at 5.2 s, the run losing its follow (exit 111), the job without its summary and the socket left. The rule. daemon.shutdown-grace-seconds defaults to 60, under a session's 90 s, its registry text saying why, and the planner's pinned draft digest moves with it. A user unit's 5 s, which the unit's TimeoutStopSec=infinity cannot lengthen, is issue 7's, opened for section 5's design.

Issue 12, found while building S4: what issues 5 and 6 left unsaid, agreed. On the lab of section 4, A and B two builds of the tree and N the tree at 0.30.0 and IPC schema 13: B's run beside A's idle daemon drained it and started B's in the same invocation, exit 0; beside A running a job it was refused, daemon_draining (exit 112), and so was A's own run, A exiting by itself at its job's end; N drained and replaced B's idle daemon, of an older schema to N; and B beside N's daemon was refused. Four things the issues did not say were settled in the build. The rule. A drained daemon holding no job that has not exited within daemon.start-timeout refuses the submission in issue 5's layout (in the unit test: a daemon exits within milliseconds):

daemon_draining: no job running, not exited after 5s; none submitted; karvi daemon stop, or --no-daemon
  daemon build 9Au3WHU839F5xE-67H93 0.29.0
  client build XfTtoeK1qq9HkGakcVu7 0.29.0

A daemon of a newer IPC schema, whose build the client cannot read, is refused with the schemas stacked as the builds are, and job follow and job cancel beside a daemon of another schema name the karvi that speaks it, an older daemon's by the version it answered with, a newer one's as the newer karvi:

daemon_incompatible: the daemon speaks a newer IPC schema; none submitted; karvi daemon stop from the newer karvi, or --no-daemon
  daemon ipc_schema 13
  client ipc_schema 12
daemon_incompatible: the daemon speaks an older IPC schema; follow its jobs with karvi 0.29.0
  daemon ipc_schema 12
  client ipc_schema 13

The dry run's daemon line and the exercise report's name the daemon's build after its version, so that daemon_other_build's finding is read against it: daemon local: incompatible pid=… version=0.29.0 build=XvVcKUyDY-UJ1l3tZw8E ipc_schema=12. An interrupt during the sequence's wait, for a drained daemon to exit or a launched one to answer, is cancelled (exit 113), nothing having been submitted, where it was daemon_start_failed (112). A daemon of another build that prepares the job a second time, after the sequence has run once more, is refused daemon_incompatible, its registry text saying so. Not taken: the client stopping a drained daemon that has not exited (a client drains and never stops); the schemas on one line (two values compared are read stacked); the client's build on the dry run's line (karvi version names it); a code of its own for the second prepare (three daemons in one invocation, the code that names a daemon the client will not use).

Issue 13, found while designing section 5: the unit's kill mode, agreed. The packaged unit names no KillMode, so systemd's control-group applies: a stop sends SIGTERM to every process of the unit at once, the daemon and each OpenSSH process of the system transport. On a transient user service with TimeoutStopSec=infinity, as issue 7 has the unit, a 12-second job (sleep 12; echo done) through the system transport to 127.0.0.1 and the unit stopped 3 s into it: under control-group the stop returned at 3.1 s, the job's session lost (command_session_lost, exit 101, its summary errored); under KillMode=mixed, SIGTERM to the daemon alone and SIGKILL to what remains once it has exited, the job ran to its end, exit 0, completed, the stop returning at 12.3 s; under mixed with a grace of 4 the daemon cancelled and recorded it at 7.1 s, incomplete_shutdown (exit 106), nothing left in the unit's cgroup. A job of the native transport has no child and drained under either, as on issue 7's lab. The rule. The packaged daemon unit sets KillMode=mixed beside TimeoutStopSec=infinity: a stop or a restart signals the daemon alone, which drains and ends its own sessions within its grace, and systemd kills what remains after it exits; the unit's comment says so. A login session's scope, where a client-launched daemon runs, stops by control-group too, so at a shutdown, or a logout that ends the user's processes, that daemon's jobs of the system transport end at once, recorded as on the service; no unit of karvi's sets that scope. Not taken: control-group (every OpenSSH session cut with the daemon's SIGTERM, the drain defeated for the system transport); process (the daemon alone signalled, and what it leaves keeps running after it).

Issue 14, the service account's daemon at a host's shutdown, agreed. Issue 11 found a user unit's daemon under user@.service's 5-second stop at a shutdown, which the unit's TimeoutStopSec=infinity cannot lengthen. With netops.test standing for the service account, a 12-second job on the fake device, and the stop 3 s into it. A user unit (TimeoutStopSec=infinity, KillMode=mixed) under the account's user manager, the manager stopped as a shutdown stops it: the daemon killed 5 s later, the run losing its follow (follow_stream_lost, exit 111), the job without its summary, d.sock left. The same with a drop-in for that account's manager alone raising its stop to 90 s: the job completed, exit 0; but a drop-in named karvi-s5.conf sorted before Ubuntu's timeout.conf, which won, and the stop stayed 5 s, where one of the same name or sorting after it took. A transient system unit, User=netops.test, TimeoutStopSec=infinity, KillMode=mixed, Wants= and After=network-online.target, no user manager and no linger: the stop waited, and the job completed, exit 0, at 12.7 s. network-online.target is loaded and active in the system manager and not-found in a user manager, so the packaged user unit's After= ordered nothing. Found: logind's RemoveIPC=yes, the default, removes every file an account owns under /dev/shm 10 s (UserStopDelaySec) after its last logind session ends, a user manager started for it counting as one: a first run of the system unit's case lost its running job's manifest and commands.jsonl so, the job ending errored. System users are excluded (logind.conf(5)), a lingering user is never finalized, and sudo -u opens no session. The rule. A service account's daemon runs under a system unit: the package ships the template karvi-daemon@.service, User=%i, which the administrator enables for the account, systemctl enable --now karvi-daemon@ACCOUNT.service, started after the network, stopped at a shutdown before the network goes, and bounded by the daemon's grace alone (TimeoutStopSec=infinity, KillMode=mixed), with Restart=on-failure and the idle timer off as before, and no user manager or linger. The account is a system user (adduser --system), whose files logind leaves alone. The packaged user unit karvi-daemon.service is removed. Issue 7's words move with it: no loginctl enable-linger and no systemctl --user; daemon status names unit: karvi-daemon@ACCOUNT.service; daemon stop and daemon restart refuse naming systemctl restart of that unit; and the crun unit's comment names the template. An operator's own daemon is the client's launch (issues 1 to 6); an operator who kept one through a logout under the user unit on a host that ends users' processes at logout no longer can. Not taken: the user unit as issue 7 had it (killed 5 s into every shutdown on Ubuntu); the user unit with a drop-in for the account's user manager (three things to set, a drop-in's name that loses without a word, and no order against the network); a packaged drop-in for every user's manager (every user's shutdown changed). Noted, not opened: an operator's own daemon running after the operator's last logout loses the operator's files under /dev/shm, the scoreboards among them, 10 s later.

Issue 15, how a client knows a daemon is systemd's, agreed. Issue 7 has a client never drain a unit's daemon: drained, the daemon exits 0, which Restart=on-failure does not restart, so after an install the account's first run would leave it without a daemon until the administrator acted. Neither the daemon's pid nor its parent tells who started it: a client's launch from a login session has PID 1 for its parent, as a system unit's daemon has, and a client's launch from a user service's shell the user manager, as a user unit's daemon has. The daemon's environment and cgroup would tell it (INVOCATION_ID set and the cgroup ending in the unit's .service, where a client's launch, which filters the environment, has no INVOCATION_ID), at the price of the daemon reading both and its answer naming its unit and its manager. The client's own configuration says it more plainly. The rule. daemon.launch names who starts the account's daemon, a word, as display.color takes one: "client", the default, the client starting its own daemon as issues 1 to 6 have it, for an operator; "systemd", set for the service account by the install script, or locked for every operator of a host in the site's global file, the daemon started by the unit karvi-daemon@USER.service, USER the client's own account. Under "systemd" a client sends no drain and starts no daemon: a daemon of its own build not draining takes the submission; a daemon of another build refuses it, never drained, in issue 5's layout:

daemon_incompatible: the daemon runs another build; none submitted; systemctl restart karvi-daemon@karvi.service, or --no-daemon
  daemon build 9Au3WHU839F5xE-67H93 0.29.0
  client build XfTtoeK1qq9HkGakcVu7 0.29.0

A draining daemon, stopping under its unit, refuses it daemon_draining as in issue 5; with no daemon the submission and daemon start are refused daemon_unreachable, naming systemctl start of the unit; daemon stop and daemon restart are refused with a new code, daemon_managed:

daemon_managed: daemon.launch is systemd; systemctl stop karvi-daemon@karvi.service

The dry run reports a daemon of another build as an error, and daemon status's remediation names the unit's restart. The daemon is unchanged: it names no unit and reads neither its environment nor its cgroup, which replaces issue 7's unit: line, its INVOCATION_ID, and its boolean daemon.launch. Not taken: the daemon knowing its unit from INVOCATION_ID and its cgroup (a reading and two fields where the client's setting says it); the parent pid (above); daemon.launch a boolean (false says what a client does not do, not who does); "systemd-managed" (the key already says launch); the refusals naming "its unit" (the install script enables exactly karvi-daemon@USER.service).

Issue 16, the service account's install and its removal, agreed. What it gains: the service account's daemon under systemd set up on a host in one command, run as root, and undone in one, where OPERATIONS would otherwise give the administrator steps to follow and to remember for their undoing. It waits on issues 14 and 15, the template unit and daemon.launch = "systemd", and is built with them. Found while designing it: a home at /var/lib/karvi, one of karvi's two system roots, owned by the account at 0700, would refuse other operators' runs, which look for /var/lib/karvi/shared and /var/lib/karvi/users/<username> there and pass by an absent one but not one they may not read (internal/osutil/paths.go, read, not run). The rule. A bash script, karvi-sysutil, in the tree's packaging/sbin/ and installed as /usr/sbin/karvi-sysutil with a manual page karvi-sysutil(8), its commands naming their object as karvi's do: sudo karvi-sysutil daemon install [--group NAME] and sudo karvi-sysutil daemon remove [--purge]. It runs as root alone, directly or through sudo, and exits 1 otherwise. daemon install, in order: runs karvi setup shared --group NAME, which reports each place exists on a host already set up, so that the site's shared trees and /opt/karvi/users exist, every operator's jobs, collections, and transcripts then going to the trees under /opt/karvi/shared; makes the account karvi, a system user (issue 14) with its group, its home /opt/karvi/users/karvi, which is also its private root, and no login shell, an existing account of that name taken only when it is a system user; adds it to the operators' group, netops unless --group names another, a group that does not exist refused; writes the account's /opt/karvi/users/karvi/.config/karvi/config.toml, its own and mode 0600, with [daemon] launch = "systemd", a file already there refused, never edited; checks as the account that karvi config show daemon.launch says systemd, so that a global file setting config.allow-user-layer = false is found; checks that the package's template unit is installed, refusing without it, and runs systemctl enable --now karvi-daemon@karvi.service; checks karvi daemon status as the account; each part entered in the record /etc/karvi/service-karvi.installed as it is made or found (below). The record states that the unit runs the daemon as the account with the configuration karvi reads for it, the global file (/etc/karvi/config.toml, else /opt/karvi/config.toml, the first that exists) and then the account's own, named by its full path, and lists what the install made as against what it found. daemon remove undoes what the record lists, in reverse: systemctl disable --now of the instance, the stop waiting for its jobs under the daemon's grace as at a shutdown; and the configuration file and the group membership, each when the install made it. The account and its home stay, and the record keeps their lines; under --purge the account's files in the shared trees and the shared scratch go to root, and the account with its home, when the install made it, and the record go too (below). What the install found is left, and so are the template unit, the package's, and the shared trees, which hold every operator's work. karvi reads no configuration file in a private root: only the global file, the account's ~/.config/karvi/config.toml, and --config. Not taken: the home /var/lib/karvi (above); karvi-service (at the operator's word, a name for root's system tasks); the bare install and remove (read as installing karvi); the script in /usr/share/karvi (not on root's path) or scripts/ (the tree's test and release tools, not shipped); karvi setup service in Go (the operator's bash, its work useradd and systemctl).

Issue 16, continued: the template unit's place, agreed. What it gains: the unit an instance runs is the installed release's, where a copy the install made into /etc/systemd/system from /usr/share/karvi/systemd/system would keep its settings through every later upgrade without a word, this chapter's own KillMode and TimeoutStopSec among them. It waits on nothing. The rule. The package installs the template where systemd finds a package's units, /usr/lib/systemd/system/karvi-daemon@.service, the one unit it installs there, and an upgrade replaces it. A template runs nothing until an instance is enabled, so nothing the package installs is active until the site chooses, as before. The package never enables, starts, or restarts an instance (issue 7): dh_installsystemd makes no maintainer-script snippet for a unit named with @ (/usr/bin/dh_installsystemd, read, not run), so the rules need no override. A site's own settings go in a drop-in, /etc/systemd/system/karvi-daemon@.service.d/, which an upgrade never touches. karvi-sysutil copies no unit: daemon install refuses without the template (karvi-daemon@.service not installed; install the karvi package), and daemon remove leaves it. An install from the release bundle places the file as BUILD-HOWTO section 10 says. Not taken: the install's copy into /etc/systemd/system (stale after every upgrade); systemctl link (a second way of placing a unit for the administrator to recognise); daemon install run again after each upgrade to refresh the copy (a step to forget).

Issue 16, continued: the reload at an upgrade, agreed. What it gains: the administrator's restart after an upgrade runs the new release's unit. It waits on the template's place (above). Found: an upgrade replaces the template's file and systemd keeps the unit it loaded. On a runtime user template standing for it, its file replaced while an instance ran: a restart without a daemon-reload warned that the file had changed on disk and ran the old unit, its KillMode and its command; a daemon-reload alone gave the running instance the new KillMode at once, its pid the same, and the new command at its next restart. dh_installsystemd makes no reload for a template, and systemd's dpkg triggers watch no unit directory. The rule. The package runs systemctl --system daemon-reload itself, as debhelper's snippets do for any other unit: packaging/debian/karvi.postinst at configure and dpkg's abort- cases, packaging/debian/karvi.postrm at remove, each when systemd runs (/run/systemd/system exists) and DPKG_ROOT is empty, a failure ignored. It enables, starts, and restarts nothing. After an upgrade the administrator's one step is systemctl restart karvi-daemon@karvi.service. Not taken: the administrator's own daemon-reload before the restart (forgotten, the restart runs the old unit with a warning alone).

Issue 16, continued: what a removal keeps, and --purge, agreed. What it gains: no later account takes over the service account's files. It waits on nothing. Found: with the shared trees in place, the account's home holds its configuration, its trust store, and the daemon's log and state; its jobs, collection files, and transcripts are in the shared trees, and its scoreboards, the ledger files it last wrote, and its scratch in the shared scratch, all owned by its uid (FILES section 2). adduser --system gives a new account the lowest free uid from 100 (first_avail_uid in /usr/sbin/adduser, read, not run): an account removed hands its uid to the next system account a package makes, which would own the service account's jobs and collections, devices' configurations among them, and could open them to anyone. The rule. daemon remove stops and disables the instance and removes the configuration file and the group membership, each when the install made it; the account and its home stay, its files keeping their owner, and a later daemon install takes the account back with its trust store; the record keeps its lines for the account and its home, so that a later daemon remove --purge knows the install made them. daemon remove --purge does the same, then gives every file the account owns in the shared trees and the shared scratch to root, its group and mode unchanged, so that the operators read them still and no later account owns them; removes the account and its home, when the install made it; and removes the record. Not taken: the account removed by a plain daemon remove (its uid reused); its files left in the shared trees under a removed uid at --purge (the same); its files there removed at --purge (other operators' jobs sit in day folders it made, and they are the site's records).

Issue 16, continued: an install or a removal run again, agreed. What it gains: an install that failed partway is finished by running it again, or undone, where a record written last would leave none: its account taken as found by the next run, and so never removed, and its configuration file refused. It waits on nothing. The rule. The install enters each part in the record as it makes or finds it, so that a failed install leaves the list of what it did. daemon install reads the record: each part it lists is checked and reported exists, a part missing is made, and the two checks, daemon.launch and daemon status, always run; run again on a whole install, it changes nothing and reports each part exists, as karvi setup shared does, and after a plain daemon remove it takes the account back and makes the rest. A configuration file the record does not list is refused, as before. A --group other than the one whose membership the record lists is refused, the two stacked, naming daemon remove. daemon remove reports a part already gone absent, and without a record is refused: no service installed: /etc/karvi/service-karvi.installed absent. Not taken: a second install refused outright (a failed one cleaned by hand); the record written last (above).

Issue 16, continued: no dry run, and an install that repairs, agreed. What it gains: a refused install leaves the host as it was, and an install run over an existing one sets right what has drifted from what it made. It waits on nothing. The rule. karvi-sysutil has no --dry-run: daemon install checks every refusal before it changes anything (root, the template installed, the group, an account karvi that is not a system user, a configuration file the record does not list, a --group other than the record's, the recorded uid held by another account), so that a refusal changes nothing and a failure partway is an error it did not foresee, which the record covers; the manual page lists the steps. Over an existing install, daemon install repairs each part the record lists as made: a part missing is made again, the account with the uid the record holds, so that its files keep their owner; the account's home and login shell, the home's owner and mode, the configuration file's owner and mode (the account's, 0600), and the group membership are set back; and the instance is enabled and started when it is not, after systemctl reset-failed when it failed. Each part is reported made, exists, or repaired, a repaired part's value as found over the value set, stacked. It repairs neither the configuration file's content, which the administrator may have added to (a daemon.launch other than systemd is refused, stacked, naming the file), nor a running daemon of another build, which waits on the administrator's restart (issue 7), the status naming it. Not taken: --dry-run (a second path through the script to keep in step with the first, its answer only as good as what it can check before anything is made); a repair that rewrites the configuration file (the administrator's additions lost); a repair that restarts the daemon (the restart's moment the administrator's).

Issue 16, continued: the test, in a container, agreed. What it gains: karvi-sysutil, the package's template and maintainer scripts, and the account's daemon tested as root on an Ubuntu 26.04 host that is not this one, at the operator's word, so that nothing here needs sudo or changes. It waits on the S5 build. Found: ubuntu:26.04 (Ubuntu 26.04.1 LTS, systemd 259.5) with systemd its PID 1 under Docker 29.8.2, without --privileged; as Docker runs it, systemd exits 255, Failed to create /init.scope control group: Read-only file system, and with --cap-add SYS_ADMIN alone the same; with SYS_ADMIN, AppArmor unconfined, a private cgroup namespace, and an entrypoint that remounts /sys/fs/cgroup writable, it runs with no unit failed, logind and sshd up. In it, logind removed an ordinary user's /dev/shm file 13 s after the user's ssh session ended and kept a system user's, and docker stop, systemd's halt signal, waited out a unit's 8-s stop, systemd exiting 0, as on this host (issue 14). The rule. scripts/sysutil-smoke-test.sh PACKAGE builds the image (ubuntu:26.04 with systemd, logind, adduser, sudo, and OpenSSH), boots it with no network, installs PACKAGE with dpkg -i, and runs: the refusals (not root, no template, the group missing, a karvi account not a system user, a configuration file the record does not list), each exiting 1 with the accounts, /opt/karvi, and /etc/karvi as before; the install, each part made, daemon.launch systemd as the account, and daemon status running; the install again, each part exists and nothing changed; the repair, the membership, the configuration file's mode, and the instance broken and the account removed, each repaired or made, the account with its uid; a --group other than the record's, refused, stacked; the shutdown, a 12-s job and docker stop 3 s into it, the job's summary after docker start saying it completed; RemoveIPC, an operator's ssh login and logout, the account's files under /dev/shm there 13 s later; the upgrade, the installed template changed by hand and PACKAGE installed again, the instance no longer needing a reload and its daemon's pid the same; the removal, the account and its home kept, absent at a second removal, the account taken back by an install, --purge giving its shared files to root and removing it, and a removal without a record refused; and dpkg -r, the template gone and systemd reloaded. It removes its container and image at the end, and touches no other. Every connection is to the container's own sshd on 127.0.0.1, which needs no network; the image is built with one, for its packages. The unit's order against the network is the one thing the container cannot show, with a network or without: Docker sets up its interface outside its systemd. The test reads the unit's Wants= and After= instead, systemd stopping units in the reverse of their start order. It is run by hand at S5's end and by verify-release.sh beside check-deb.sh (S6); the battery does not run it, since it needs Docker and a package. Not taken: the test with sudo on this host (the host changed); LXD (here only its installer, which needs sudo and adds a bridge, NAT rules, and a storage pool to this host); --privileged (every capability and the host's devices, where one capability and the cgroup's remount do); a network in the container (it reaches this host and what this host reaches, the site's devices among them, and tests nothing loopback does not).

Issue 17, found while building S5, agreed but its first point. On S5's lab builds, the client's (5a429b3-s5a) and the package's (5a429b3-s5b), the battery and scripts/sysutil-smoke-test.sh passed. The points. (1) A daemon of a newer IPC schema under daemon.launch = "systemd", which issue 15 left unsaid: built refusing the submission, daemon start, and the dry run, daemon_incompatible, the schemas stacked, naming systemctl restart of the unit, where karvi daemon stop from the newer karvi would be refused daemon_managed; its review is the next issue. (2) karvi-sysutil reports a part it makes created, the word karvi setup shared writes in the same output above it; the record keeps made and found. (3) An existing account karvi is taken only when it is a system user whose home is /opt/karvi/users/karvi, since karvi reads the account's configuration from the home the password database names; another home is refused, the two stacked. (4) --purge removes the account's own spool directories, /tmp/karvi-UID and /var/tmp/karvi-UID, with its home. (5) The package depends on adduser, whose adduser, addgroup, deluser, and delgroup the script runs. (6) The template keeps the user unit's other settings, NoNewPrivileges=yes, its limits, UMask=0077, and no file-system sandbox, the daemon writing where its clients read; under it a job's folder was 2750 and its files 0640 in the operators' group, as FILES says. (7) The test's job runs on karvi's fake device, built from the tree, and the operator logs in through the container's sshd, both on its 127.0.0.1. Found by the test: the account made again with its recorded uid, 100 in the container, by adduser --system --group --uid 100 failed, groupadd: GID '100' already exists, Ubuntu's users: the script makes the account's group first, addgroup --system, at a gid of its own, and the account in it. Issue 17, continued: the first point, agreed. On S5a's lab build beside a fixture daemon of IPC schema 13, the client's 12: under daemon.launch = "client" daemon start was refused naming karvi daemon stop from the newer karvi; under "systemd" naming systemctl restart of the unit, and daemon stop was refused daemon_managed; exit 112 throughout. Under "systemd" the unit runs /usr/bin/karvi, so a daemon newer than the client follows a rollback (BUILD-HOWTO section 10, apt install --allow-downgrades), its daemon running the newer program until restarted, or a client that is not the installed karvi. The rule, as built: the refusal names systemctl restart of the unit, which stops the daemon under its grace whatever its schema and starts the installed karvi; for a client that is not the installed karvi the stacked schemas show it. Not taken: the client shape's karvi daemon stop from the newer karvi (refused daemon_managed, and gone after a rollback); the client reading the daemon's /proc/PID/exe to tell a rollback from another copy (a reading for a rare case).

Issue 18: the jump box's rollback, not taken; the idle timer's default 10m. On the lab, build N's daemon (0.30.0, IPC schema 13) ran a 12 s job beside S5a's karvi (0.29.0, schema 12), its operator's own under "client": run was refused naming karvi daemon stop from the newer karvi, which the rollback removed; daemon status said daemon not running or unreadable; daemon stop and daemon stop --grace were refused ipc_schema_incompatible; exit 112 throughout. A SIGTERM to the socket's listener drained the daemon, the job completed with exit 0, and the older karvi then launched its own. The gap considered: daemon stop reaching a newer daemon by signal, its pid from the socket's peer credentials. Not taken, on the owner's word: an operator does not downgrade; the administrator does, and ends the running daemons or restarts the host. In its place, daemon.shutdown-idle-timer's default becomes 10m from 1h, so that after an upgrade or a downgrade an idle daemon of the old build is gone within some ten minutes of its last job, at the cost of a relaunch of some 80 ms after a longer pause. The unit's timer stays off.
